Definition 4.3.1 . The upper bound on the selective classification performance for a fixed full-coverage accuracy a full ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] a_{\text{full}}\in[0,1] and a variable coverage level c ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] c\in[0,1] is given by acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) = { 1 0 &lt; c ≤ a full a full c a full &lt; c &lt; 1 . \overline{\text{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c)=\begin{cases}1&amp;0&lt;c\leq a_{\text{full}}\\ \frac{a_{\text{full}}}{c}&amp;a_{\text{full}}&lt;c&lt;1\end{cases}. (4.5) Measuring the area enclosed between the bound acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) \overline{\text{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c) and an SC method’s achieved accuracy/coverage trade-off acc c ​ ( f , g ) \text{acc}_{c}(f,g) yields our accuracy-normalized selective classification score. Definition 4.3.2 . The accuracy-normalized selective classification score s a full ​ ( f , g ) s_{a_{\text{full}}}(f,g) for a selective classifier ( f , g ) (f,g) with full-coverage accuracy a full a_{\text{full}} is given by s a full ​ ( f , g ) = ∫ 0 1 ( acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ​ ( f , g ) ) ​ 𝑑 c ≈ ∑ c ( acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ​ ( f , g ) ) . s_{a_{\text{full}}}(f,g)=\int_{0}^{1}(\overline{\text{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c)-\text{acc}_{c}(f,g))dc\approx\sum_{c}(\overline{\text{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c)-\text{acc}_{c}(f,g)). (4.6) We provide additional intuition as part of Figure 4.2 . Bound Justification. To understand the upper bound given in Equation 5.5 , note that a full-coverage accuracy level of a full a_{\text{full}} means that a fraction a full a_{\text{full}} of points are correctly classified. An ideal selective classification method for this model would, by increasing the coverage c c from 0 to 1, first accept all points that the model classifies correctly: this gives the optimal selective classification accuracy for coverage c ≤ a full c\leq a_{\text{full}} of 100 % 100\% . For any coverage level c &gt; a full c&gt;a_{\text{full}} , note that the accuracy at the coverage is the number of correct points accepted divided by the total number of points accepted. The largest value this can take is by maximizing the numerator, and the maximum value for this (as a fraction of all points) is a full a_{\text{full}} . Plugging this into the previous statement for accuracy at coverage c c , we have the best selective accuracy at a coverage c c is a full c \frac{a_{\text{full}}}{c} . This derives the upper bound stated above as a function of coverage. We remark that this bound is in fact achievable if a selective classifier separates correct and incorrect points perfectly, i.e., it accepts all correct points first and then accepts and increasing amount of incorrect points as we increase the coverage of the selective classification method. See Appendix B.2.3 for an experiment that matches the bound exactly. Figure 4.3 : Accuracy-coverage trade-off across datasets &amp; ε \varepsilon levels . We highlight noteworthy results for SCTD, DE, and SN with bold lines and further show that SN only provides results at coarse-grained coverage levels. SCTD performs best while DE and SN performance is compromised at any ε &lt; ∞ \varepsilon&lt;\infty . 4.4 Experiments We now present a detailed experimental evaluation of the interplay between differential privacy and selective classification. All of our experiments are implemented using PyTorch ( Paszke et al. 2019 ) and Opacus ( Yousefpour et al. 2021 ) . We publish our full code-base at the following URL: https://github.com/cleverhans-lab/selective-classification . Setup. Our evaluation is based on the following experimental panel. In particular, we conduct experiments for each dataset/SC-method/ ε \varepsilon combination: • Datasets : FashionMNIST ( Xiao et al. 2017 ) , CIFAR-10 ( Krizhevsky et al. 2009 ) , SVHN ( Netzer et al. 2011 ) , GTSRB ( Houben et al. 2013 ) . • Selective Classification Methods : Softmax Response (SR) ( Geifman &amp; El-Yaniv 2017 ) , SelectiveNet (SN) ( Geifman &amp; El-Yaniv 2019 ) , Self-Adaptive Training (SAT) ( Huang et al. 2020 ) , Monte-Carlo Dropout (MC-DO) ( Gal &amp; Ghahramani 2016 ) , Deep Ensembles (DE) ( Lakshminarayanan et al. 2017b ) , Selective Classification Training Dynamics (SCTD) ( Rabanser et al. 2025c ) . • Privacy levels : ε ∈ { ∞ , 7 , 3 , 1 } \varepsilon\in\{\infty,7,3,1\} . Based on recent results from Feng et al. 2023 , we (i) apply additional entropy regularization to all methods; and (ii) derive the selective classification scores for SN and SAT by applying Softmax Response (SR) to the underlying classifier (instead of relying on model-specific abstention mechanisms). Across all private experiments, we set δ = 10 − ( N + 1 ) \delta=10^{-(N+1)} for datasets consisting of training samples on the order of 10 N 10^{N} . For each combination of SC method, dataset, and privacy level, we train a model following the ResNet-18 ( He et al. 2016 ) model architecture using either the SGD optimizer (for ε = ∞ \varepsilon=\infty ) or the DP-SGD optimizer (for all ε &lt; ∞ \varepsilon&lt;\infty ). All models are trained for 200 epochs using a mini-batch size of 128. For all DP training runs, we set the clipping norm to c = 10 c=10 and choose the noise multiplier adaptively to reach the overall privacy budget at the end of training. Note that, since we want to consider a consistent value of ε \varepsilon across all SC methods, we restrict the overall ε \varepsilon in both SN and DE. As explained in Section 4.3.2 , this effectively enforces a reduction in ε \varepsilon for each individual training run due to sequential composition. Concretely, we train DE with 5 ensemble members and SN with target coverage levels c target ∈ { 0.1 , 0.25 , 0.5 , 0.75 , 1.0 } c_{\text{target}}\in\{0.1,0.25,0.5,0.75,1.0\} . This leads to a reduction of ≈ ε 5 \approx\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{5}} for each trained sub-model in both cases. We account for the precise reduction in the privacy parameter by increasing by the number steps of DP-SGD as measured by a Rényi DP accountant ( Mironov 2017 ; Yousefpour et al. 2021 ) . All experiments are repeated over 5 random seeds to determine the statistical significance of our findings. We document additional hyper-parameters in Appendix B.2.1 . Table 4.1 : Coverage required for non-private full-coverage accuracy. We observe that differential privacy considerably lowers the allowable coverage to achieve a utility level consistent with the non-private model. Across our experiments, SCTD yields the highest coverage across ε \varepsilon levels. We plot both the accuracy-coverage trade-off of SCTD, i.e., acc c ​ ( f , g ) \text{acc}_{c}(f,g) , with a solid line and the upper bound acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) \overline{\text{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c) with a dashed line. The shaded region enclosed between the two curves corresponds to the accuracy-normalized SC score s a full ​ ( f , g ) s_{a_{\text{full}}}(f,g) . We observe that the score grows with stronger DP levels (shown by brighter colors), i.e., selective classification becomes harder at low ε \varepsilon s. Recovering Non-Private Utility by Reducing Coverage. Recall that one key motivation for applying selective classification to a DP model is to recover non-private model utility at the expense of coverage. We investigate this coverage cost in detail by examining how many samples a DP model can produce decisions for while maintaining the utility level of the respective non-private model (i.e., a model trained on the same dataset without DP). The results are outlined in Table 4.1 . For high (i.e., ε = 7 \varepsilon=7 ) and moderate (i.e., ε = 3 \varepsilon=3 ) privacy budgets, we observe that a reduction of 20 % − 30 % 20\%-30\% in data coverage recovers non-private utility. However, at low (i.e., ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 ) privacy budget we observe that dataset coverage degrades strongly on most datasets. In particular, we find that for CIFAR-10 (across all ε \varepsilon values) and GTSRB (at ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 ), the coverage reduces to below 30 % 30\% . This result showcases that, depending on the particular choice of ε \varepsilon , recovering non-private model performance from a DP model can come at a considerable coverage cost. Moreover, this coverage cost varies widely across SC methods with SCTD leading to the lowest incurred loss in coverage by far. Disentangling Selective Prediction Performance From Base Accuracy. Recall from Section 4.3.4 that the standard evaluation pipeline for selective classification is not suitable for comparing DP models across varying privacy guarantees. To overcome this issue, we compute the accuracy-dependent upper bound (Equation 4.5 ) for each experiment and measure how closely the achieved accuracy/coverage trade-off aligns with this bound as per Equation 4.6 . We document these results computed over all datasets, SC methods, and ε \varepsilon levels in Table 4.2 . We find that, as ε \varepsilon decreases, all methods perform progressively worse. That is, for stronger privacy, all methods increasingly struggle to identify points they predict correctly on. Recall that this behavior is expected based on our discussion in Section 4.3.3 . Again, we observe SCTD offering the strongest results, leading to the smallest bound deviation. To graphically showcase closeness to the upper bound, we further plot the accuracy-coverage trade-off and the corresponding upper bound for each experiment with the SCTD method in Figure 4.4 . Figure 4.5 : Sequential composition (DE) vs parallel composition ( DE-PART ) for deep ensembles. We observe that DE-PART (orange) under-performs DE (blue) for selective classification across all ε \varepsilon . Parallel Composition With Partitioned Ensembles. As previously stated, Deep Ensembles under-perform under DP due to the sequential composition property. One potential mitigation strategy is to partition the data and train isolated models with no data overlap. This circumvents composition but also limits the utility of individual ensemble members as less data is available for training. We experiment with both setups and report results in Figure 4.5 . Overall, these experimental results indicate that (i) partitioned deep ensembles lead to lower full-coverage accuracy when compared to non-partitioned deep ensembles while (ii) at the same time being less performant in terms of selective classification performance. 4.5 Conclusion In this work we have studied methods for performing selective classification under a differential privacy constraint. To that end, we have highlighted the fundamental difficulty of performing selective prediction under differential privacy, both via a synthetic example and empirical studies. To enable this analysis, we introduced a novel score that disentangles selective classification performance from baseline utility. While we establish that SC under DP is indeed challenging, our study finds that a specific method (SCTD) achieves the best trade-offs between SC performance and privacy budget. Limitations. This work presents insights drawn from empirical results and we acknowledge that a more thorough theoretical analysis is needed for a deeper understanding of the interplay between SC and DP. Also, we did not carefully investigate fairness implications beyond class imbalance. This connection to fairness requires special attention with past works showing that both DP and SC can negatively affect sensitive subgroups ( Jones et al. 2020 ; Bagdasaryan et al. 2019 ) . Future Work. We believe that this work initiates a fruitful line of research. In particular, future work can expand on the intuition provided here to obtain fundamental bounds on selective classification performance in a DP setting. Although we did not focus on this here, we believe that the ideas developed in this chapter could help mitigate the trade-offs between privacy and fairness. Chapter 5 What Does It Take to Build a Performant Selective Classifier? Paper Reference . The contents of this chapter consist of research and results taken from: Rabanser &amp; Papernot 2025 : Summary Selective classifiers improve reliability by abstaining on uncertain inputs, yet their performance often lags behind the perfect-ordering oracle that accepts examples in exact order of correctness. We formulate this shortfall as a coverage‑uniform selective‑classification gap and prove the first finite‑sample decomposition that pinpoints five distinct sources of looseness: Bayes noise, approximation error, ranking error, statistical noise, and implementation or shift‑induced slack. Our bound shows that monotone post‑hoc calibration cannot reduce the gap, as it preserves the original score ordering; closing the gap therefore requires scoring mechanisms that can modify the ranking induced by the base model. We validate our gap decomposition on synthetic two‐moons data and real‐world vision benchmarks, isolating each error component via controlled experiments. Results confirm that (i) Bayes noise and limited model capacity alone explain large gaps, (ii) only non‑monotone or feature‑aware calibrators shrink the ranking term, and (iii) distribution shift adds a distinct slack that must be addressed by robust training. Together, our decomposition supplies a quantitative error budget and concrete design guidelines for building selective classifiers that approach ideal oracle behavior. 5.1 Introduction In high-stakes applications like finance ( Coenen et al. 2020 ) , healthcare ( Guan et al. 2020 ) , and autonomous driving ( Ghodsi et al. 2021 ) , machine learning (ML) models are increasingly tasked with making decisions under uncertainty, where dependable predictions are critical. Selective classifiers ( Chow 1957 ; El-Yaniv et al. 2010 ) formalize the option to abstain on inputs deemed unreliable, reducing the risk of costly errors by refusing to predict when uncertain. Their effectiveness depends on identifying which predictions to trust and which to defer. A common evaluation metric is the accuracy–coverage tradeoff, which quantifies how performance degrades as the model accepts a broader set of inputs. The benchmark is a hypothetical oracle that ranks inputs by their true likelihood of correctness, yielding a perfect-ordering upper bound ( Geifman et al. 2018 ; Rabanser et al. 2023 ) . While some models approach this bound, others fall short—revealing persistent gaps and raising open questions about what properties of the learning setup truly govern selective performance. 0 0 0.2 0.2 0.4 0.4 0.6 0.6 0.8 0.8 1 1 0 0 0.5 0.5 1 1 a full a_{\text{full}} c = a full c=a_{\text{full}} correct points incorrect points Coverage ( c c ) Selective Accuracy acc c ​ ( h , g ) \text{acc}_{c}(h,g) acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) \overline{\text{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c) ∫ 0 1 Δ ^ ​ ( c ) ​ 𝑑 c \int_{0}^{1}\widehat{\Delta}(c)dc Figure 5.1 : Selective classification gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) . The dashed curve is the oracle frontier acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) \overline{\mathrm{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c) under which coverage levels left of c = a full c=a_{\text{full}} ( blue ) accept correct predictions first, and rank incorrect predictions last ( orange ). The solid curve shows the realized selective accuracy acc c ​ ( h , g ) \mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g) . The mismtach between acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) \overline{\mathrm{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c) and acc c ​ ( h , g ) \mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g) at coverage c c is the gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) ; the gray area visualizes the gap area over the full coverage spectrum. Classical theory explains selective classification in two idealized regimes. In the realizable setting ( El-Yaniv et al. 2010 ) , where the data is noiseless and the true predictor lies within the hypothesis class, the model can asymptotically achieve the perfect accuracy–coverage curve. In the more general agnostic setting ( Wiener &amp; El-Yaniv 2011 ) , the classifier competes with the best-in-class predictor, but this benchmark may itself fall well below the oracle bound—and the theory does not isolate the source of the gap. As a result, even the strongest formal guarantees offer little practical guidance: For my finite model on finite data, what aspects of the learning setup will actually move my trade-off curve closer to the upper bound? To answer this question, we re‑frame selective performance around the selective classification gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) : the mismatch between a model’s accuracy–coverage curve and the oracle bound (see Figure 5.1 for an illustrative example). We show that this gap admits a finite‑sample decomposition: Δ ^ ​ ( c ) ≤ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) ⏟ irreducible + ε approx ​ ( c ) ⏟ capacity + ε rank ​ ( c ) ⏟ ranking + ε stat ​ ( c ) ⏟ data + ε misc ​ ( c ) ⏟ optimization &amp; shift , ∀ c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] . \widehat{\Delta}(c)\;\leq\;\underbrace{\varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c)}_{\text{\scriptsize irreducible}}+\;\underbrace{\varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c)}_{\text{\scriptsize capacity}}+\;\underbrace{\varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)}_{\text{\scriptsize ranking}}+\;\underbrace{\varepsilon_{\text{stat}}(c)}_{\text{\scriptsize data}}+\;\underbrace{\varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c)}_{\text{\scriptsize optimization\;\&amp;\;shift}},\qquad\forall c\in(0,1]. (5.1) Each term corresponds to a distinct—and often measurable —source of looseness. The first term, ε Bayes ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c) , reflects irreducible uncertainty: if the true label is inherently unpredictable from the input (e.g., due to label noise), even a perfect classifier must abstain on some examples. Next, ε approx ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c) captures limits of the model class: if the function class is too weak to approximate the Bayes-optimal decision rule, the gap widens. The third term, ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) , quantifies the model’s failure to correctly order inputs by their likelihood of correctness—typically due to poor confidence estimation or miscalibration. The statistical term ε stat ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{stat}}(c) accounts for finite-sample fluctuations that affect both learning and evaluation. Finally, ε misc ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c) aggregates practical imperfections, such as optimization error or test-time distribution shift. Equation ( 5.1 ) thus provides a coverage-uniform “error budget” that transforms the qualitative question posed earlier into a concrete quantitative diagnosis. Two insights, developed further in later sections, are worth previewing. First, monotone post‑hoc calibration cannot reduce the ranking term ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) , as it preserves the total order of scores—leaving the accuracy–coverage curve unchanged. Second, Equation ( 5.1 ) serves as an error budget that identifies cost‑effective levers: (i) increase capacity or distill from a more expressive teacher to shrink ε approx \varepsilon_{\text{approx}} ; (ii) use additional or repeated labels and noise‑robust losses to reduce ε Bayes \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}} ; (iii) enlarge validation data to lower ε stat \varepsilon_{\text{stat}} ; and (iv) apply domain adaptation or importance weighting to address ε misc \varepsilon_{\text{misc}} . Contributions. We summarize our main contributions below: • Problem formulation. We recast selective prediction in terms of a coverage‑uniform selective‑classification gap —the key quantity to minimize to approach oracle behavior. This framing unifies prior work and highlights which failure modes dominate at each coverage level. • Theoretical analysis. We present the first finite‑sample decomposition of the gap (Eq. ( 5.1 )), dividing it into five terms: Bayes, approximation, ranking, statistical, and miscellaneous errors. Our analysis further shows that monotone calibration cannot reduce the gap , motivating ranking‑aware methods. • Empirical validation. Experiments—from two‑moons to large‑scale vision—confirm the decomposition: Bayes noise and capacity limits drive large gaps; temperature scaling improves calibration but not ranking; and shift-aware methods remain essential under distribution shift. These results clarify which factors matter most and how to target them effectively in practice. 5.2 Background &amp; Related Work Selective classification extends the standard supervised classification framework as follows: Definition 5.2.1 (Selective Classifier ( Chow 1957 ; El-Yaniv et al. 2010 ) ) . A selective classifier is a pair ( h , g ) (h,g) , where h : 𝒳 → 𝒴 h:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y} is a classifier over covariates 𝒳 = ℝ D \mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{D} and labels 𝒴 = { 1 , … , K } \mathcal{Y}=\{1,\ldots,K\} , and g : 𝒳 × ( 𝒳 → 𝒴 ) → ℝ g:\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y})\to\mathbb{R} is a selection function that assigns a confidence score. Given a threshold τ ∈ ℝ \tau\in\mathbb{R} , the model abstains when the score falls below the threshold: ( h , g ) ​ ( x ) = { h ⁡ ( x ) if ​ g ​ ( x , h ) ≥ τ ⊥ otherwise . (h,g)(x)=\begin{cases}h(x)&amp;\text{if }g(x,h)\geq\tau\\ \bot&amp;\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\kern 5.0pt. (5.2) Intuitively, a selective classifier predicts only when confident. The selection score g ⁡ ( x , h ) g(x,h) determines whether to accept or abstain: if g ⁡ ( x , h ) ≥ τ g(x,h)\geq\tau , the model outputs h ⁡ ( x ) h(x) ; otherwise, it returns ⊥ \bot . Many prior works have developed selective classification methods for training competitive pairs ( h , g ) (h,g) . A popular selective prediction method is Softmax Response (SR) ( Hendrycks &amp; Gimpel 2016 ; Geifman &amp; El-Yaniv 2017 ) , which uses classifier confidence as the selection score. To improve calibration and reduce predictive variance, ensembling approaches have been explored: Deep Ensembles (DE) ( Lakshminarayanan et al. 2017b ) train multiple models with different initializations, while Selective Classification via Training Dynamics (SCTD) ( Rabanser et al. 2025c ) ensembles intermediate checkpoints. Other methods—such as SelectiveNet (SN) ( Geifman &amp; El-Yaniv 2019 ) , Deep Gamblers (DG) ( Liu et al. 2019 ) , and Self-Adaptive Training (SAT) ( Huang et al. 2020 ) —alter the model architecture or loss function ensuring that prediction and rejection are learned jointly. The efficacy of a selective classifier is evaluated using the empirical accuracy-coverage tradeoff. Definition 5.2.2 (Empirical Accuracy–Coverage Tradeoff) . Let D = { ( x i , y i ) } i = 1 N D=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N} be a dataset. For a selective classifier ( h , g ) (h,g) and threshold τ \tau , define ξ ^ h , g ​ ( τ ) \displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{h,g}(\tau) = 1 N ​ | { i : g ⁡ ( x i , h ) ≥ τ } | , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\,\bigl|\{\,i:g(x_{i},h)\geq\tau\}\bigr|, (5.3) α ^ h , g ​ ( τ ) \displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{h,g}(\tau) = { | { i : h ⁡ ( x i ) = y i ​ and ​ g ​ ( x i , h ) ≥ τ } | | { i : g ⁡ ( x i , h ) ≥ τ } | , if ​ ξ ^ h , g ​ ( τ ) &gt; 0 , 0 , if ​ ξ ^ h , g ​ ( τ ) = 0 . \displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\bigl|\{\,i:h(x_{i})=y_{i}\text{ and }g(x_{i},h)\geq\tau\}\bigr|}{\bigl|\{\,i:g(x_{i},h)\geq\tau\}\bigr|},&amp;\text{if }\hat{\xi}_{h,g}(\tau)&gt;0,\\[10.0pt] 0,&amp;\text{if }\hat{\xi}_{h,g}(\tau)=0.\end{cases} (5.4) The pair ( ξ ^ , α ^ ) (\hat{\xi},\hat{\alpha}) as τ \tau varies is the empirical accuracy–coverage curve. The score g ⁡ ( x , h ) g(x,h) therefore induces a total order over D D : x 1 x_{1} is accepted before x 2 x_{2} if g ⁡ ( x 1 , h ) &gt; g ⁡ ( x 2 , h ) g(x_{1},h)&gt;g(x_{2},h) . This ordering governs which inputs are retained as coverage decreases. Effective strategies aim to maximize α ^ \hat{\alpha} at each coverage level ξ ^ \hat{\xi} , often trading off accuracy and coverage. Accuracy–coverage tradeoff evaluation. The accuracy–coverage tradeoff is often summarized by the area under the accuracy–coverage curve ( AUACC ), integrating selective accuracy over all coverage levels. However, Geifman et al. 2018 show that AUACC favors models already accurate at full coverage. To address this issue, Geifman et al. 2018 and Rabanser et al. 2023 propose oracle-based bounds, which become loose at low utility ( Galil et al. 2023 ) . To avoid accuracy bias, Galil et al. 2023 and Pugnana &amp; Ruggieri 2023 recommend using the classifier’s AUROC instead. But AUROC is not monotonic in AUACC ( Cattelan &amp; Silva 2023a ; Ding et al. 2020 ) , thus favoring methods tuned for AUROC over selective accuracy. Earlier work ( El-Yaniv et al. 2010 ; Wiener &amp; El-Yaniv 2011 ) characterizes optimal selective classifiers in both realizable and agnostic regimes but focuses on existence rather than practical instantiation—unlike our finite-sample perspective. 5.3 Decomposing the Selective Classification Gap To Optimality We characterize the optimal performance achievable by a selective classifier given its full-coverage accuracy, establishing a reference against which all practical selective classifiers can be evaluated. 5.3.1 Oracle Bound and Selective Classification Gap Definition 5.3.1 (Perfect Ordering Upper Bound ( Geifman et al. 2018 ; Rabanser et al. 2023 ) ) . Fix a base classifier h h whose full‑coverage (standard) accuracy is a full := Pr ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( X ) = Y ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] a_{\text{full}}:=\Pr\bigl(h(X)=Y\bigr)\in[0,1] . For any desired coverage level c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] c\in(0,1] , the best selective accuracy—achieved by accepting the c c -fraction of points with the highest posterior correctness Pr ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( X ) = Y ∣ X ) \Pr(h(X)=Y\mid X) —is acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) = { 1 , 0 &lt; c ≤ a full , a full c , a full &lt; c &lt; 1 . \overline{\mathrm{acc}}\bigl(a_{\text{full}},c\bigr)=\;\begin{cases}1,&amp;0&lt;c\leq a_{\text{full}},\\[6.0pt] \dfrac{a_{\text{full}}}{c},&amp;a_{\text{full}}&lt;c&lt;1.\end{cases} (5.5) Assuming no Bayes noise—that is, all errors are avoidable given perfect confidence—this piecewise curve (see Figure 5.1 ) traces the oracle accuracy–coverage frontier based on a perfect ranking of examples by correctness probability. Any real selective classifier falls below this bound—potentially far below, depending on its calibration, expressivity, and sensitivity to noise. To quantify how far a given classifier falls short of this ideal, we define the selective classification gap . Definition 5.3.2 (Selective Classification Gap) . Let ( h , g ) (h,g) be a selective classifier with full‑coverage accuracy a full = Pr ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( X ) = Y ) a_{\mathrm{full}}=\Pr(h(X)=Y) . For a coverage level c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] c\in(0,1] , let τ c \tau_{c} be the threshold satisfying Pr ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( X , h ) ≥ τ c ) = c \Pr\bigl(g(X,h)\geq\tau_{c}\bigr)=c . The selective accuracy at coverage c c is acc c ​ ( h , g ) := Pr ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( X ) = Y | g ⁡ ( X , h ) ≥ τ c ) . \mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g):=\Pr\bigl(h(X)=Y\;\bigm|\;g(X,h)\geq\tau_{c}\bigr). The selective classification gap at coverage c c is then defined as the deviation from the perfect-ordering upper bound: Δ ⁡ ( c ) := acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ​ ( h , g ) . \Delta(c):=\overline{\mathrm{acc}}\bigl(a_{\mathrm{full}},c\bigr)\;-\;\mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g). (5.6) This gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) represents the excess selective risk at a given coverage c c . Its integral over the entire coverage spectrum, ∫ 0 1 Δ ⁡ ( c ) ​ 𝑑 c \int_{0}^{1}\Delta(c)dc , is equivalent to the Excess-AURC (E-AURC) metric proposed by Geifman et al. 2018 . The function Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) offers a coverage-resolved diagnostic of selective performance. A small gap indicates near-oracle behavior—accepting only examples it can confidently and correctly classify—while a large gap suggests limitations in estimating correctness or ranking examples reliably. Understanding the magnitude and shape of this gap is key to analyzing and improving selective classifiers. 5.3.2 Why Is the Upper Bound Loose? The oracle bound in Definition 5.3.1 relies on two idealized assumptions: perfect prediction on all inputs and perfect ranking by the true correctness posterior. In practice, selective classifiers deviate in four principal ways, each corresponding to a term in our later decomposition ( ε Bayes , ε approx , ε rank , ε stat \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}},\varepsilon_{\text{approx}},\varepsilon_{\text{rank}},\varepsilon_{\text{stat}} ): 1. Bayes noise ( ε Bayes \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}} ). Even a Bayes-optimal rule errs on intrinsically ambiguous points (where max y ⁡ Pr ⁡ ( Y = y ∣ X ) &lt; 1 \max_{y}\Pr(Y=y\mid X)&lt;1 ), unavoidable in real data ( Devroye et al. 2013 ) . As coverage increases, the oracle must accept some of these noisy inputs, lowering the achievable accuracy. 2. Approximation limits ( ε approx \varepsilon_{\text{approx}} ). A learned model h h drawn from a restricted hypothesis class may misclassify inputs with high posterior confidence under the Bayes rule ( Bishop 2006 ) . This gap reduces full-coverage accuracy and limits selective performance. 3. Ranking error ( ε rank \varepsilon_{\text{rank}} ). Let η h ​ ( x ) := Pr ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( x ) = Y ∣ X = x ) \eta_{h}(x):=\Pr\bigl(h(x)=Y\mid X=x\bigr) denote the true correctness posterior. The confidence score g ⁡ ( X , h ) g(X,h) may mis-order inputs relative to η h ​ ( x ) \eta_{h}(x) , leading easy examples to be rejected and harder ones accepted, which increases Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) . 4. Statistical noise ( ε stat \varepsilon_{\text{stat}} ). Estimating the threshold τ c \tau_{c} and selective accuracy from a finite validation set introduces randomness of order 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) / n ) \mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}) . This follows from concentration bounds; see Shalev-Shwartz &amp; Ben-David 2014 for standard applications in learning theory. Takeaway . The selective classification gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) reflects a mix of irreducible noise, model misspecification, ranking errors, and sampling variability. Addressing each—via cleaner labels, stronger models, or improved ranking—can tighten selective prediction performance. Next, we formalize this decomposition and provide a general bound on the total gap. 5.3.3 Formal Decomposition of the Gap We now give a principled decomposition of the selective classification gap and provide a corresponding finite-sample upper bound. For clarity and notational simplicity, we treat the binary‑label case 𝒴 = { 0 , 1 } \mathcal{Y}=\{0,1\} ; the multiclass extension follows by a standard one‑vs‑rest reduction. Notation. Let η ⁡ ( x ) := Pr ⁡ ( Y = 1 ∣ X = x ) \eta(x):=\Pr\bigl(Y=1\mid X=x\bigr) be the Bayes posterior. For a fixed classifier h : 𝒳 → 𝒴 h:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y} define its (induced) correctness posterior η h ( x ) := Pr ( h ( x ) = Y ∣ X = x ) = η ( x ) 𝕀 { h ( x ) = 1 } + ( 1 − η ( x ) ) 𝕀 { h ( x ) = 0 } . \eta_{h}(x)\;:=\;\Pr\bigl(h(x)=Y\mid X=x\bigr)\;=\;\eta(x)\,\mathbb{I}_{\{h(x)=1\}}+\bigl(1-\eta(x)\bigr)\mathbb{I}_{\{h(x)=0\}}. (5.7) All expectations and probabilities are taken w.r.t. the true data distribution 𝒟 \mathcal{D} . Throughout let g ⁡ ( x , h ) g(x,h) be the confidence score. For a target coverage c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] c\in(0,1] denote by t c s.t. Pr ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( X , h ) ≥ t c ) = c t_{c}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\Pr\bigl(g(X,h)\geq t_{c}\bigr)=c (5.8) the population threshold , and write the accepted region A c := { x : g ⁡ ( x , h ) ≥ t c } A_{c}:=\{x:g(x,h)\geq t_{c}\} . The oracle that attains the perfect‑ordering bound accepts A c ⋆ := { x : η h ​ ( x ) ​ is among the largest ​ c ​ -fraction } A_{c}^{\star}:=\bigl\{x:\eta_{h}(x)\text{ is among the largest }c\text{-fraction}\bigr\} . Error Terms. We isolate the following sources of error affecting selective prediction performance: ε Bayes ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c) : = 𝔼 ⁡ [ 1 − max ⁡ { η ⁡ ( X ) , 1 − η ⁡ ( X ) } | X ∈ A c ] , \displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[1-\max\{\eta(X),1-\eta(X)\}\;\Bigm|\;X\in A_{c}\Bigr], (5.9) ε approx ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c) : = 𝔼 ⁡ [ | η h ​ ( X ) − η ⁡ ( X ) | | X ∈ A c ] , \displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl|\eta_{h}(X)-\eta(X)\bigr|\;\Bigm|\;X\in A_{c}\Bigr], (5.10) ε rank ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) : = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ∣ X ∈ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ∣ X ∈ A c ] ( ≥ 0 ) , \displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\eta_{h}(X)\mid X\in A_{c}^{\star}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\eta_{h}(X)\mid X\in A_{c}\bigr]\;\;\;\;\;\;(\geq 0), (5.11) ε stat ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\text{stat}}(c) : = C ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) n , \displaystyle:=C\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}, (5.12) where n n is the evaluation‑set size, δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) a confidence parameter, and C &gt; 0 C&gt;0 an absolute constant. Intuitively, ε Bayes \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}} is the irreducible label noise inside the accepted region; ε approx \varepsilon_{\text{approx}} measures how far h h is from Bayes‑optimal on the selected inputs; and ε rank \varepsilon_{\text{rank}} is a ranking regret —the accuracy loss due solely to picking the wrong c c -fraction of examples. Remark 5.3.3 (Distance to a Perfect Ranker) . A natural way to gauge how far the learned acceptance rule is from the oracle is the mass mis-ordered D rank ​ ( c ) := Pr ⁡ ( X ∈ A c ⋆ ∖ A c ) + Pr ⁡ ( X ∈ A c ∖ A c ⋆ ) . D_{\text{rank}}(c)\;:=\;\Pr\bigl(X\in A_{c}^{\star}\setminus A_{c}\bigr)+\Pr\bigl(X\in A_{c}\setminus A_{c}^{\star}\bigr). (5.13) It equals the total probability of examples that would have to be swapped between A c A_{c} and A c ⋆ A_{c}^{\star} to recover perfect ordering. Hence D rank ​ ( c ) = 0 D_{\text{rank}}(c)=0 iff A c = A c ⋆ A_{c}=A_{c}^{\star} , in which case ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) also vanishes. Theorem 5.3.4 (Selective Gap Bound) . For a coverage level c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] c\in(0,1] and a selective classifier ( h , g ) (h,g) the population gap obeys Δ ⁡ ( c ) = acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ​ ( h , g ) ≤ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) + ε approx ​ ( c ) + ε rank ​ ( c ) . \Delta(c)=\overline{\mathrm{acc}}\bigl(a_{\mathrm{full}},c\bigr)-\mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g)\;\leq\;\varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c). (5.14) Let Δ ^ ​ ( c ) \widehat{\Delta}(c) be the empirical gap on n n i.i.d. test points. Then, with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , Δ ^ ​ ( c ) ≤ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) + ε approx ​ ( c ) + ε rank ​ ( c ) + C ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) n . \widehat{\Delta}(c)\;\leq\;\varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)+C\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}. (5.15) Proof. Because acc c ​ ( h , g ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ∣ A c ] , \mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g)=\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}(X)\mid A_{c}], the gap decomposes as Δ ⁡ ( c ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ] ⏟ ε rank ​ ( c ) + 𝔼 [ η h − 𝕀 { h = Y } ∣ A c ] ⏟ ε approx ​ ( c ) + 𝔼 ⁡ [ 1 − max ⁡ { η , 1 − η } ∣ A c ] ⏟ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) . \Delta(c)=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}]}_{\varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)}\;+\;\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}-\mathbb{I}_{\{h=Y\}}\mid A_{c}]}_{\varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c)}\;+\;\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[1-\max\{\eta,1-\eta\}\mid A_{c}]}_{\varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c)}. This yields the population bound ( 5.14 ). For each expectation in the decomposition apply Hoeffding’s inequality, a union bound over the three terms gives, with probability 1 − δ 1-\delta , | Δ ^ ​ ( c ) − Δ ⁡ ( c ) | ≤ C ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) / n . |\widehat{\Delta}(c)-\Delta(c)|\leq C\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}. Adding this deviation to ( 5.14 ) establishes ( 5.15 ). See Appendix C.2.1 for an extended proof with detailed intermediate steps. ∎ 5.3.4 Calibration and Its (Limited) Effect on the Gap As shown in Theorem 5.3.4 , the selective classification gap includes a ranking error term ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) , which captures misalignment between the confidence score and true correctness. Model calibration ( Niculescu-Mizil &amp; Caruana 2005 ) —widely used to reduce over- or underconfidence—is often assumed to improve this alignment by transforming scores to better reflect correctness likelihood. Yet its effect on selective performance remains ambiguous and context-dependent. Prior work has reached conflicting conclusions: Zhu et al. 2022 argue that calibration may degrade abstention behavior, while Galil et al. 2023 find that temperature scaling can improve selective prediction in practice. We show that the impact on the gap depends critically on the type of calibration method used and its influence on the induced ranking. We begin by recalling the formal definition of calibration. Definition 5.3.5 (Perfect Calibration) . For each input x x let a model produce a predicted label y ^ ​ ( x ) \hat{y}(x) and an associated confidence score s ⁡ ( x ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] s(x)\in[0,1] . We say the model is perfectly calibrated if Pr ⁡ ( Y = y ^ ​ ( X ) | s ⁡ ( X ) = t ) = t for every confidence level ​ t ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] . \Pr\bigl(Y=\hat{y}(X)\;\bigm|\;s(X)=t\bigr)\;=\;t\qquad\text{for every confidence level}\ t\in[0,1]. (5.16) Practical estimators approximate ( 5.16 ) via a post-hoc map ϕ \phi such that s ~ ​ ( x ) = ϕ ​ ( s ​ ( x ) ) \tilde{s}(x)=\phi(s(x)) approaches prefect calibration. Expected Calibration Error (ECE) ( Naeini et al. 2015 ) quantifies this closeness: ECE = ∑ b = 1 B | I b | n | 1 | I b | ∑ i ∈ I b 𝕀 { y ^ ( x i ) = y i } − 1 | I b | ∑ i ∈ I b s ~ ( x i ) | , \text{ECE}=\sum_{b=1}^{B}\frac{|I_{b}|}{n}\left|\frac{1}{|I_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I_{b}}\mathbb{I}\{\hat{y}(x_{i})=y_{i}\}-\frac{1}{|I_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I_{b}}\tilde{s}(x_{i})\right|, (5.17) where I b I_{b} is the set of indices in bin b b , n n is the total number of examples, and B B is the number of bins. Monotone Score-Level Calibration Leaves the Gap Intact. Temperature scaling ( Guo et al. 2017 ) , isotonic regression ( Zadrozny &amp; Elkan 2002 ) , and histogram binning ( Zadrozny &amp; Elkan 2001 ) all fit a monotone ϕ : [ 0 , 1 ] → [ 0 , 1 ] \phi\colon[0,1]\to[0,1] that preserves score ordering. Because monotone maps preserve ordering, the acceptance set A c = { x : s ~ ​ ( x ) ≥ τ c } A_{c}=\{x:\tilde{s}(x)\geq\tau_{c}\} is identical to the one obtained from s ⁡ ( x ) s(x) ; hence the selective accuracy acc c ​ ( h , g ) \mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g) and the gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) = acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ​ ( h , g ) \Delta(c)=\overline{\mathrm{acc}}\bigl(a_{\text{full}},c\bigr)-\mathrm{acc}_{c}(h,g) are unchanged . Monotone calibration thereby reduces the approximation error ε approx ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c) in Section 5.3.3 but leaves the ranking error ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) untouched. Why Temperature Scaling Can Reorder But Rarely Does. Temperature scaling, the most widely used post-hoc calibration technique, divides every logit vector z ⁡ ( x ) ∈ ℝ K z(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{K} by a scalar T &gt; 0 T&gt;0 , p j ( T ) ​ ( x ) = exp ⁡ ( z j ​ ( x ) / T ) ∑ k exp ⁡ ( z k ​ ( x ) / T ) . p_{j}^{(T)}(x)=\frac{\exp(z_{j}(x)/T)}{\sum_{k}\exp(z_{k}(x)/T)}. (5.18) Two inputs with different logit margins can swap their max-probability after rescaling. In theory this breaks the monotone ranking guarantee; in practice such ties occur only when the runner-up logit gaps are extremely small. Consequently, the induced change in the accuracy–coverage curve is negligible—a pattern we also observe empirically. See Appendix C.2.2 for an extended discussion. Moving the Gap Requires Non-Monotone Scoring. To reduce the selective classification gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) , one must go beyond simple monotone calibration and actively modify the acceptance ordering through one of the following approaches: • Adaptive, ensemble, or feature-aware scoring : Techniques such as self-adaptive training ( SAT ), deep ensembles ( DE ), and learned calibration heads g ψ ​ ( x ) g_{\psi}(x) predict correctness scores from hidden representations or multiple model votes. These approaches leverage instance-specific uncertainty and shared feature context to rerank samples that otherwise receive similar raw confidence values. • Binning or resampling : Techniques such as Bayesian Binning into Quantiles (BBQ) ( Naeini et al. 2015 ) and conformal p-value methods ( Angelopoulos &amp; Bates 2021 ) refine confidence scores by estimating accuracy over adaptive data subsets. Both use empirical statistics—either at the bin level or per example—to recalibrate and reorder predictions, often improving ranking. • Vector/Dirichlet scaling : A non-monotone transformation of the logit vector z z via a learned affine map W ​ z + b Wz+b , with W ∈ ℝ K × K W\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K} , enables reordering of confidence scores by capturing inter-class dependencies, nonlinear score interactions, and representation-sensitive shifts ( Kull et al. 2019 ) . Loss Prediction as a Multicalibration Litmus Test. A complementary view on how calibration connects to ranking ability starts from multicalibration ( Hébert-Johnson et al. 2018 ) , the degree to which a model is calibrated on select subgroups. Recent work by Gollakota et al. 2025 shows that strong multicalibration is closely tied to a model’s ability to predict its own loss , an idea seemingly closely related to selective prediction. We formalize this connection in Appendix C.5 and show, both theoretically and empirically, that success or failure at the loss-prediction task corresponds directly to the magnitude of the ranking error ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) . In short, if a model’s confidence scores cannot be out-predicted on their own mistakes, they are effectively multicalibrated—and near the oracle frontier. Takeaway . Monotone score-level calibration (e.g., temperature scaling) is not enough to reduce the selective classification gap. Closing the gap requires scoring methods that actively change the ranking—through feature-aware heads, ensembles, or non-monotone transformations. 5.3.5 Additional Practical Sources of Looseness The decomposition in Theorem 5.3.4 captures the intrinsic sources of error—Bayes noise, approximation limits, ranking error, and sampling slack—forming a principled bound that holds even under perfect optimization, infinite data, and i.i.d. testing. In practical deployments, however, additional imperfections can inflate the empirical gap Δ ^ ​ ( c ) \widehat{\Delta}(c) . These stem from implementation details, scoring granularity, and distribution shift—not fundamental limits, but contingent slack terms reducible through better engineering. We summarize them below under a single residual slack term ε misc ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c) . 1. Optimization error ε opt \varepsilon_{\text{opt}} . In practice, gradient‐based solvers rarely attain the empirical risk minimizer. If L ⁡ ( θ ) L(\theta) denotes the end-to-end training objective—encompassing model architecture, loss (e.g. cross-entropy), and training data—and θ ^ \hat{\theta} its final iterate, then ε opt = L ⁡ ( θ ^ ) − min θ ⁡ L ⁡ ( θ ) \varepsilon_{\text{opt}}\;=\;L(\hat{\theta})\;-\;\min_{\theta}L(\theta) , which—via standard surrogate‐to‐0/1 calibration bounds—translates into a nonzero selective‐accuracy loss that persists even under infinite data. 2. Distribution shift ε shift ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{shift}}(c) . When the test distribution p test p_{\mathrm{test}} deviates from the training distribution p train p_{\mathrm{train}} , both calibration and ranking typically degrade. In particular, for a hypothesis class ℋ \mathcal{H} , the gap due to shift can be bounded by an Integral Probability Metric (IPM) ( Müller 1997 ) : ε shift ​ ( c ) ≤ IPM ℋ ​ ( p train , p test ) := sup f ∈ ℋ | 𝔼 p train ​ [ f ] − 𝔼 p test ​ [ f ] | . \varepsilon_{\text{shift}}(c)\;\leq\;\mathrm{IPM}_{\mathcal{H}}\bigl(p_{\mathrm{train}},\,p_{\mathrm{test}}\bigr):=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{p_{\mathrm{train}}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{p_{\mathrm{test}}}[f]\right|. (5.19) Hence, larger shifts in distribution (relative to ℋ \mathcal{H} ) lead to wider selective classification gaps. Residual Slack. We absorb remaining implementation‐level effects (threshold‐selection noise, score quantization, etc.) into ε misc ​ ( c ) := ε opt + ε shift ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c):=\varepsilon_{\text{opt}}+\varepsilon_{\text{shift}}(c) , yielding the streamlined high‐probability bound. Δ ^ ​ ( c ) ≤ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) + ε approx ​ ( c ) + ε rank ​ ( c ) + ε stat ​ ( c ) ⏟ intrinsic + ε misc ​ ( c ) . \widehat{\Delta}(c)\;\leq\;\underbrace{\varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{approx}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{stat}}(c)}_{\text{intrinsic}}\;+\;\varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c). (5.20) Takeaway . Only ε Bayes \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}} reflects irreducible uncertainty; the other intrinsic terms— ε approx \varepsilon_{\text{approx}} , ε rank \varepsilon_{\text{rank}} , and ε stat \varepsilon_{\text{stat}} —can be reduced with better models, calibration, and data. The miscellaneous slack ε misc \varepsilon_{\text{misc}} highlights optimization and shift-robustness as levers for closing the gap to the oracle. 5.4 Empirical Results Our experimental study is organized around three guiding questions that reflect the theoretical decomposition in Section 5.3 . Unless otherwise specified, all selective‑accuracy curves are averaged over 5 random seeds. By default, we use a ResNet-18 as the predictive model and adopt maximum softmax probability (MSP) as the selection mechanism—due to its simplicity and widespread use. 5.4.1 Q1: How do Bayes error and approximation error shape the gap? Setup. We conduct both synthetic and real-world experiments. For our synthetic results, which give us precise control over the data generation process, we simulate two sources of intrinsic difficulty on the two‑moons dataset: (i) noise σ ∈ { 0.1 , 0.33 , 0.66 , 1.5 } \sigma\in\{0.1,\,0.33,0.66,\,1.5\} controls how much the two moons expand into each other; and (ii) model capacity , varied from logistic regression (low capacity) to a shallow MLP (high capacity). For our real-world experiments we tackle the analysis similarly: for (i) we evaluate a trained CIFAR-10 model on the CIFAR-10N/100N ( Wei et al. 2021 ) datasets to assess which data points have large labeling disagreement; and for (ii) we vary the model architecture across a simple CNN (details in Appendix C.4.3 ), a ResNet-18 ( He et al. 2016 ) , and a WideResNet-50 ( Zagoruyko &amp; Komodakis 2016 ) on CIFAR-100 ( Krizhevsky et al. 2009 ) and StanfordCars ( Krause et al. 2013 ) . For each setting, we plot and compute the Excess-AURC (E-AURC) ( Geifman et al. 2018 ) by integrating the empirical gap Δ ^ ​ ( c ) \widehat{\Delta}(c) across all coverage levels. In terms of approximation error, Figure 5.2 demonstrates that limited model capacity leads to larger gaps, while more expressive models yield tighter alignment with the perfect-ordering bound. This suggests that approximation error is a key driver of looseness. In terms of Bayes error, Figure 5.3 shows that increasing label noise consistently shifts the accuracy–coverage curve downward, indicating that Bayes error introduces an irreducible component to the gap. These results validate the canonical bound (Eq. 5.15 )—large Bayes or approximation error can explain substantial looseness. Table 5.1 : Experiments on calibration across model classes on CIFAR-100 .
