For Self-Adaptive Training (SAT), we set the pre-training epochs to 100 100 and momentum parameter 0.9 0.9 . For Selective Classification Training Dynamics (SCTD), we set the weighting parameter k = 3 k=3 and consider checkpoints at a 50 50 batch resolution. For Monte-Carlo Dropout, we set the dropout probability to 0.1 0.1 . Entropy regularization as suggested in Feng et al. 2023 is employed with β = 0.01 \beta=0.01 . B.2.2 Class Imbalance Experiments We provide additional experiments on the effect of class imbalance to extend our intuition from Section 4.3.3 . To that end, we take two data sets from our main results, namely CIFAR-10 and FashionMNIST, and produce four alternate datasets from each dataset. These datasets feature various degrees of class imbalance with p 0 ∈ { 0.5 , 0.25 , 0.1 , 0.01 } p_{0}\in\{0.5,0.25,0.1,0.01\} specifying the sampling probability for class 0 0 . All other classes maintain a sampling probability of 1 1 . We then train the same model as described in Section 4.4 and apply the softmax response SC algorithm. We document these results in Figures B.1 and B.2 . For ε = ∞ \varepsilon=\infty , we observe the expected gains from SC: minority points are accepted towards the end of the coverage spectrum ( Jones et al. 2020 ) and correct points are accepted first. This effect is independent of the sampling probability p 0 p_{0} . As we decrease the ε \varepsilon budget we observe that (i) the acceptance of minority groups starts to spread over the full coverage spectrum; (ii) the accuracy on the subgroup increasingly deteriorates with smaller ε \varepsilon , and (iii) wrongful overconfidence on the minority reverses the acceptance order at low sampling probabilities p 0 p_{0} (i.e., incorrect points are often accepted first). These results indicate that employing selective classification on private data can have unwanted negative effects in the presence of subgroups. Future work should investigate this connection more thoroughly. Figure B.1 : Inducing a class imbalance on CIFAR-10 . We train multiple CIFAR-10 models across privacy levels and sampling probabilities for class 0 given by p 0 p_{0} . We plot the accuracy/coverage trade-off as well at the exact coverage level at which any point from the minority class is accepted. The accuracy-coverage trade-off for the full dataset is given by the dashed line while the trade-off for the minority group only is given by the solid line. Blue vertical lines show correctly classified points, orange points show incorrectly classified points. Non-private models accept correct points first and do so at the end of the coverage spectrum (i.e., majority points are accepted first). As we increase privacy (i.e., decrease ε \varepsilon ), the model is increasingly unable to rank minority examples based on prediction correctness and even accepts incorrect points first. Moreover, the accuracy of the model on the minority class decreases with stronger DP. These effects are especially strong for small sampling probabilities. Figure B.2 : Inducing a class imbalance on FashionMNIST . Same insights as in Figure B.2 . B.2.3 Upper Bound Reachability In Equation 5.5 , we have introduced an upper bound on the selective classification performance on a model with full-coverage accuracy a full a_{\text{full}} . We now present a simple experimental panel across varying full-coverage accuracy levels showing that this bound is in fact reachable by a perfect selective classifier. We assume a binary classification setting for which we generate a true label vector 𝒚 ∈ { 0 , 1 } n 0 + n 1 \bm{y}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{0}+n_{1}} with balanced classes, i.e., n 0 = n 1 n_{0}=n_{1} where n 0 n_{0} corresponds to the number points labeled as 0 0 and n 1 n_{1} corresponds to the number points labeled as 1 1 . Then, based on a desired accuracy level a full a_{\text{full}} , we generate a prediction vector 𝒑 \bm{p} which overlaps with 𝒚 \bm{y} for a fraction of a full a_{\text{full}} . Finally, we sample a scoring vector 𝒔 \bm{s} where each correct prediction is assigned a score s i ∼ 𝒰 0 , 0.5 s_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}_{0,0.5} and each incorrect prediction is assigned a score s i ∼ 𝒰 0.5 , 1 s_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}_{0.5,1} . Here, 𝒰 a , b \mathcal{U}_{a,b} corresponds to the uniform distribution on the interval [ a , b ) [a,b) . This score is clearly optimal since thresholding the scoring vector 𝒔 \bm{s} at 0.5 0.5 perfectly captures correct/incorrect predictions: all s i &lt; 0.5 s_{i}&lt;0.5 correspond to a correct prediction, while all s i ≥ 0.5 s_{i}\geq 0.5 correspond to an incorrect prediction. Computing the accuracy/coverage trade-off of this experiment, across various utility levels, matches the bound exactly. Figure B.3 : Upper bound matching experiment . The experiment as described in Section B.2.3 matches the optimal bound exactly across multiple full-coverage accuracy levels. Chapter C What Does It Take to Build a Performant Selective Classifier? C.1 Broader Impact This work introduces a decomposition of the selective-classification gap into measurable components—Bayes noise, approximation error, ranking error, statistical noise, and deployment slack—offering practical guidance for improving abstaining classifiers. By diagnosing which source dominates in a given setting, our method supports more targeted model design and evaluation. Positive Implications. Our decomposition improves transparency and supports safer deployment in high-stakes domains by helping practitioners understand whether their model underperforms due to ranking, capacity, or robustness. Because each gap component is explicitly quantified, our approach can serve as a tool for model debugging, monitoring, and fairer benchmarking. Potential Risks. Selective classifiers may disproportionately defer on certain groups, amplifying disparities—a risk previously observed by Jones et al. 2020 . Additionally, institutions may exploit uncertainty estimates to justify strategic abstention —deliberately deferring on individuals they prefer not to serve ( Rabanser et al. 2025b ) . While our framework identifies which part of the gap drives poor performance, it does not control how deferred inputs are handled. Mitigations. We recommend reporting gap components disaggregated by sensitive attributes, auditing scoring functions for spurious correlations, and documenting fallback policies. These steps are essential to ensure that abstention mechanisms improve reliability without undermining fairness. Outlook. We hope this work encourages more precise evaluations of selective classifiers, shifting focus from aggregate calibration to interpretable, component-wise gap analysis that can inform both technical improvements and policy safeguards. C.2 Methods Extension C.2.1 Detailed Proof of Theorem 5.3.4 We restate the theorem for convenience. Theorem C.2.1 (Selective classification gap; detailed) . Fix a coverage level c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] c\in(0,1] , a score function g ⁡ ( ⋅ , h ) g(\cdot,h) , and its associated population threshold t c t_{c} satisfying Pr ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( X , h ) ≥ t c ) = c \Pr\bigl(g(X,h)\geq t_{c}\bigr)=c . Define the accepted region A c := { x : g ⁡ ( x , h ) ≥ t c } A_{c}:=\{x:g(x,h)\geq t_{c}\} and the oracle region A c ⋆ := { x : η h ​ ( x ) ​ is among the largest ​ c ​ -fraction } A_{c}^{\star}:=\{x:\eta_{h}(x)\text{ is among the largest }c\text{-fraction}\} . With the error terms ε Bayes ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(c) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ 1 − max ⁡ { η ⁡ ( X ) , 1 − η ⁡ ( X ) } ∣ X ∈ A c ] , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\max\{\eta(X),1-\eta(X)\}\mid X\in A_{c}\right], (C.1) ε approx ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{approx}}(c) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ | η h ​ ( X ) − η ⁡ ( X ) | ∣ X ∈ A c ] , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\eta_{h}(X)-\eta(X)\rvert\mid X\in A_{c}\right], (C.2) ε rank ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ∣ X ∈ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ∣ X ∈ A c ] ( ≥ 0 ) , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\eta_{h}(X)\mid X\in A_{c}^{\star}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\eta_{h}(X)\mid X\in A_{c}\right]\;\;(\geq 0), (C.3) the population gap satisfies Δ ⁡ ( c ) = acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ⁡ ( h , g ) ≤ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) + ε approx ​ ( c ) + ε rank ​ ( c ) . \Delta(c)=\overline{\operatorname{acc}}\bigl(a_{\mathrm{full}},c\bigr)-\operatorname{acc}_{c}(h,g)\;\leq\;\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{approx}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c). (C.4) Moreover, let Δ ^ ​ ( c ) \widehat{\Delta}(c) be the empirical gap computed on n n independent test samples. Then for any δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \delta\in(0,1) , with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , Δ ^ ​ ( c ) ≤ ε Bayes ​ ( c ) + ε approx ​ ( c ) + ε rank ​ ( c ) + C ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) n , \widehat{\Delta}(c)\;\leq\;\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{approx}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c)+C\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}, (C.5) where C &gt; 0 C&gt;0 is an absolute constant. Proof. We split the argument into four self‑contained steps. Step 0. Oracle upper bound revisited. For completeness we justify the piecewise form of acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) \overline{\operatorname{acc}}\bigl(a_{\mathrm{full}},c\bigr) in Definition 5.3.1 . Because a full = Pr ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( X ) = Y ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ] a_{\mathrm{full}}=\Pr(h(X)=Y)=\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}(X)] , the set { x : η h ​ ( x ) = 1 } \{x:\eta_{h}(x)=1\} has probability mass at least a full a_{\mathrm{full}} . Hence an oracle that retains the highest‑confidence points achieves perfect accuracy for all coverages c ≤ a full c\leq a_{\mathrm{full}} . For c &gt; a full c&gt;a_{\mathrm{full}} , the best it can do is include only those perfect points plus a ( c − a full ) (c-a_{\mathrm{full}}) -fraction of the remaining examples, which contribute at worst zero accuracy. Therefore acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) = a full c , a full &lt; c &lt; 1 . \overline{\operatorname{acc}}(a_{\mathrm{full}},c)=\frac{a_{\mathrm{full}}}{c},\qquad a_{\mathrm{full}}&lt;c&lt;1. (C.6) Step 1. Algebraic decomposition of the gap. Recall that acc c ⁡ ( h , g ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ​ ( X ) ∣ X ∈ A c ] \operatorname{acc}_{c}(h,g)=\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}(X)\mid X\in A_{c}] . We repeatedly add and subtract the same quantity: Δ ⁡ ( c ) \displaystyle\Delta(c) : = acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − acc c ⁡ ( h , g ) \displaystyle:=\overline{\operatorname{acc}}(a_{\mathrm{full}},c)-\operatorname{acc}_{c}(h,g) = acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] + 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ] \displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{acc}}(a_{\mathrm{full}},c)-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]\;+\;\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ] \displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}] (rank) + 𝔼 [ η h − 𝕀 { h = Y } ∣ A c ] \displaystyle\quad+\,\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}-\mathbb{I}_{\{h=Y\}}\mid A_{c}] (approx+Bayes) = ε rank ​ ( c ) + ε approx ​ ( c ) + ε Bayes ​ ( c ) . \displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{approx}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(c). (C.7) Explanation of the two labelled inequalities. 1. (rank) isolates the ranking error, ε rank ​ ( c ) := 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ] \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c):=\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}] . The inequality holds because the remaining term from the previous line, acc ¯ ​ ( a full , c ) − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] \overline{\operatorname{acc}}(a_{\text{full}},c)-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}] , is a non-negative quantity that is bounded by the error sources introduced in the next step. 2. (approx+Bayes) adds and subtracts η ⁡ ( X ) \eta(X) inside the expectation, then splits the absolute value: η h − I { h = Y } = ( η h − η ) + ( η − I { h = Y } ) . \eta_{h}-I_{\{h=Y\}}=(\eta_{h}-\eta)+(\eta-I_{\{h=Y\}}). (C.8) The second summand satisfies the deterministic bound | η ( X ) − I { h = Y } | = max { η , 1 − η } − I { h = Y } ≤ 1 − max { η , 1 − η } , |\eta(X)-I_{\{h=Y\}}|=\max\{\eta,1-\eta\}-I_{\{h=Y\}}\leq 1-\max\{\eta,1-\eta\}, yielding exactly ε Bayes ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(c) . The first summand contributes ε approx ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{approx}}(c) . Step 2. Non‑negativity of ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c) . Because η h ​ ( X ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \eta_{h}(X)\in[0,1] and A c ⋆ A_{c}^{\star} contains the c c -fraction of points with the largest η h \eta_{h} -values, 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] ≥ 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ] \mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]\geq\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}] , hence ε rank ​ ( c ) ≥ 0 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c)\geq 0 as stated. Step 3. Finite‑sample deviation. Let μ ^ \widehat{\mu} be any empirical average of a [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] -valued random variable with expectation μ \mu . Hoeffding’s inequality gives Pr ⁡ ( | μ ^ − μ | &gt; ϵ ) ≤ 2 ​ e − 2 ​ n ​ ϵ 2 \Pr(\lvert\widehat{\mu}-\mu\rvert&gt;\epsilon)\leq 2e^{-2n\epsilon^{2}} . Apply this bound separately to the three empirical estimates that constitute Δ ^ ​ ( c ) \widehat{\Delta}(c) , and take a union bound with ϵ = log ⁡ ( 6 / δ ) 2 ​ n \epsilon=\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(6/\delta)}{2n}} . This yields, with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta , | Δ ^ ​ ( c ) − Δ ⁡ ( c ) | ≤ C ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) / n \lvert\widehat{\Delta}(c)-\Delta(c)\rvert\leq C\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n} for an absolute constant C C . Combining with ( C.4 ) proves ( C.5 ). Step 4. Connection to ranking distance. Define the mass of mis‑ordered points D rank ​ ( c ) := Pr ⁡ ( X ∈ A c ⋆ ∖ A c ) + Pr ⁡ ( X ∈ A c ∖ A c ⋆ ) D_{\mathrm{rank}}(c):=\Pr\bigl(X\in A_{c}^{\star}\setminus A_{c}\bigr)+\Pr\bigl(X\in A_{c}\setminus A_{c}^{\star}\bigr) . Because η h ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \eta_{h}\in[0,1] , ε rank ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ η h ∣ A c ] \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}^{\star}]-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{h}\mid A_{c}] (C.9) ≤ ‖ η h ‖ ∞ ​ D rank ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\eta_{h}\bigr\|_{\infty}\,D_{\mathrm{rank}}(c) (C.10) ≤ D rank ​ ( c ) . \displaystyle\leq D_{\mathrm{rank}}(c). (C.11) Hence ε rank ​ ( c ) = 0 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{rank}}(c)=0 if and only if A c = A c ⋆ A_{c}=A_{c}^{\star} . This completes the proof. ∎ Multiclass Remark. For K &gt; 2 K&gt;2 labels, define η ⁡ ( x ) = ( Pr ⁡ ( Y = 1 ∣ x ) , … , Pr ⁡ ( Y = K ∣ x ) ) \eta(x)=\bigl(\Pr(Y=1\mid x),\dots,\Pr(Y=K\mid x)\bigr) and its complement confidence η max ​ ( x ) = max k ⁡ η k ​ ( x ) \eta^{\max}(x)=\max_{k}\eta_{k}(x) . Then the inequality | η max − I { h = Y } | ≤ 1 − η max \lvert\eta^{\max}-I_{\{h=Y\}}\rvert\leq 1-\eta^{\max} replaces the binary bound above, and the rest of the argument goes through verbatim. The approximation term becomes 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∥ η h − η ∥ 1 ∣ A c ] \mathbb{E}[\lVert\eta_{h}-\eta\rVert_{1}\mid A_{c}] ; all other quantities are unchanged. C.2.2 When Can Temperature Scaling Re-rank Confidence Scores? Temperature scaling multiplies every logit by the same factor 1 / T ⁡ ( T &gt; 0 ) 1/T\;(T&gt;0) before the softmax, p j ( T ) ​ ( x ) = exp ⁡ ( z j ​ ( x ) / T ) ∑ k exp ⁡ ( z k ​ ( x ) / T ) . p^{(T)}_{j}(x)=\frac{\exp(z_{j}(x)/T)}{\sum_{k}\exp(z_{k}(x)/T)}. (C.12) Although the predicted label arg ⁡ max j ​ z j ​ ( x ) \arg\max_{j}z_{j}(x) is invariant to T T , the confidence score s T ​ ( x ) = max j ⁡ p j ( T ) ​ ( x ) s_{T}(x)=\max_{j}p^{(T)}_{j}(x) can change its cross-sample ordering. General form. Let j ⋆ = arg ⁡ max j ​ z j ​ ( x ) j_{\star}=\arg\max_{j}z_{j}(x) and r j ​ ( x ) = exp ⁡ ( z j ​ ( x ) − z j ⋆ ​ ( x ) ) ​ ( j ≠ j ⋆ ) r_{j}(x)=\exp\bigl(z_{j}(x)-z_{j_{\star}}(x)\bigr)\;(j\neq j_{\star}) . Then s T ​ ( x ) = 1 1 + ∑ j ≠ j ⋆ r j ​ ( x ) 1 / T . s_{T}(x)=\frac{1}{1+\sum_{j\neq j_{\star}}r_{j}(x)^{1/T}}. (C.13) For binary classification, the sum has a single term and ( C.13 ) collapses to the familiar logistic form s T ( x ) = 1 / ( 1 + e − Δ / T ) s_{T}(x)=1/(1+e^{-\Delta/T}) with Δ = z j ⋆ − z 3 − j ⋆ \Delta=z_{j_{\star}}-z_{3-j_{\star}} . Two-sample condition. For two inputs x 1 , x 2 x_{1},x_{2} let S i ​ ( T ) = ∑ j ≠ j ⋆ ( i ) r i ​ j 1 / T S_{i}(T)=\sum_{j\neq j_{\star}^{(i)}}r_{ij}^{1/T} . Because each r i ​ j ≤ 1 r_{ij}\leq 1 , every r i ​ j 1 / T r_{ij}^{1/T} is monotone non-decreasing in T T (strictly increasing unless there is a tie), and the ordering s T ​ ( x 1 ) &gt; s T ​ ( x 2 ) s_{T}(x_{1})&gt;s_{T}(x_{2}) can change exactly at those temperatures T T where S 1 ​ ( T ) = S 2 ​ ( T ) S_{1}(T)=S_{2}(T) . Illustrative example ( K = 3 K=3 ). z ( 1 ) = ( − 2 , − 3 , − 3 ) , z ( 2 ) = ( 0 , − 0.1 , − 3 ) . z^{(1)}=(-2,-3,-3),\quad z^{(2)}=(0,-0.1,-3). (C.14) At T = 1 T=1 one finds s 1 ​ ( x 1 ) = 0.576 &gt; 0.512 = s 1 ​ ( x 2 ) s_{1}(x_{1})=0.576&gt;0.512=s_{1}(x_{2}) , while at T = 3 T=3 we see that s 3 ​ ( x 1 ) = 0.411 &lt; 0.428 = s 3 ​ ( x 2 ) s_{3}(x_{1})=0.411&lt;0.428=s_{3}(x_{2}) , so temperature scaling would now accept x 2 x_{2} before x 1 x_{1} . How likely is a swap? Equation ( C.13 ) shows that a swap requires the one-dimensional curves S 1 ​ ( T ) S_{1}(T) and S 2 ​ ( T ) S_{2}(T) to intersect. Since the curves are continuous and monotone, the intersection occurs— if at all—at isolated temperatures and only when the competing logit patterns are finely tuned. Practical implication. Temperature scaling can in principle tighten the selective-classification gap, but only for the vanishingly small subset of inputs whose non-maximum logits happen to satisfy S 1 ​ ( T ⋆ ) = S 2 ​ ( T ⋆ ) S_{1}(T^{\star})=S_{2}(T^{\star}) . To obtain a meaningful re-ordering one must therefore adopt non-monotone calibration strategies. C.2.3 Additional Contingent Slack In the main text (Sec. 5.3.5 ) we folded all implementation‐level imperfections into a single residual term ε misc ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c) , retaining only optimization error and distribution shift explicitly. Here we list two further slack terms omitted there: 3. Threshold‐selection noise ε thr ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{thr}}(c) . When the coverage threshold t ^ c \hat{t}_{c} is chosen on a validation set of size m m , the realized coverage deviates from the target c c by O ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( 1 − c ) / m ) , O\bigl(\sqrt{c(1-c)/m}\bigr), (C.15) inducing a corresponding vertical shift in selective accuracy. 4. Tie‐breaking / score quantization ε tie ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{tie}}(c) . Discrete confidence values (e.g. low‐precision logits) create equivalence classes of samples with identical scores. If κ \kappa denotes the maximum number of tied samples at any score level, then ε tie ​ ( c ) ≤ κ n , \varepsilon_{\text{tie}}(c)\;\leq\;\frac{\kappa}{n}, (C.16) where n n is the size of the evaluation set. Residual slack revisited. Together with optimization error ε opt \varepsilon_{\text{opt}} and shift ε shift ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{shift}}(c) , these yield ε misc ​ ( c ) = ε opt + ε shift ​ ( c ) + ε thr ​ ( c ) + ε tie ​ ( c ) . \varepsilon_{\text{misc}}(c)=\varepsilon_{\text{opt}}+\varepsilon_{\text{shift}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{thr}}(c)+\varepsilon_{\text{tie}}(c). (C.17) C.3 Practitioner Checklist for Tightening the Selective-Classification Gap Below is an expanded, actionable checklist to help practitioners systematically tackle each component of the selective-classification gap. For each item, we list concrete steps, recommended tools, and pointers to reduce the corresponding error term. • ε approx \varepsilon_{\text{approx}} — Shrink Approximation Error – Model capacity: increase depth/width (e.g. ResNet-50→ResNeXt), or use state-of-the-art architectures (Vision Transformers, ConvNeXt). – Pre-training: leverage self-supervised (SimCLR, BYOL) or foundation models (CLIP, DINO) for strong features. – Distillation: apply teacher–student fine-tuning (logit matching, hint layers). – Data augmentation: use AutoAugment, RandAugment or MixUp / CutMix to expose model to diverse examples. • ε rank \varepsilon_{\text{rank}} — Improve Ranking Calibration: – Non-monotone recalibration: try matrix scaling or vector/Dirichlet scaling. – Feature-aware scoring: train a small “confidence head” g ψ ​ ( h ​ ( x ) , x ) g_{\psi}(h(x),x) on held-out data to predict correctness. – Conformal methods: implement conformal p-value scoring (split conformal, Mondrian conformal) to adjust rankings. – Binning approaches: use Bayesian Binning into Quantiles (BBQ) or adaptive histogram binning with bin-wise re-ordering. • ε opt \varepsilon_{\text{opt}} — Reduce Optimization Error: – Convergence diagnostics: monitor training vs. validation loss; extend epochs until plateau. – Learning-rate schedules: employ cosine, OneCycle, or cyclical LR to escape local minima. – Early stopping / checkpoints: save and ensemble top- k k checkpoints (e.g. last 5) to smooth out optimization noise. – Regularization: apply weight decay, dropout, or stochastic depth to improve generalization. • ε Bayes \varepsilon_{\text{Bayes}} — Quantify Irreducible Noise: – Repeated labels: collect multiple human annotations (e.g. CIFAR-10N) to estimate label disagreement. – Noise-robust training: use noise-aware losses (Bootstrapping, Taylor loss) when Bayes-noise is high. – Dataset curation: identify and remove or relabel ambiguous examples via active learning or consensus filtering. • ε stat \varepsilon_{\text{stat}} — Control Statistical Slack: – Validation set size: allocate a sufficiently large hold-out set (e.g. 10–20% of the total data) for threshold estimation. – Confidence intervals: use distribution-free quantile bounds (e.g. DKW, Clopper–Pearson) to set conservative τ c \tau_{c} . – Cross-validation: average thresholds over k k folds to reduce variance. • ε shift \varepsilon_{\text{shift}} — Mitigate Distribution Shift: – Shift detection: monitor covariate shifts via two-sample tests (MMD, KL divergence) between train/test features. – Importance weighting: re-weight training or calibration data by density ratio estimates (Kernel mean matching). – Domain adaptation: finetune on small in-domain samples, or use unsupervised adaptation (AdaBN, domain-Adversarial nets). – Test-time adaptation: apply batch-norm calibration or test-time training on incoming data. • ε thr \varepsilon_{\text{thr}} — Threshold–Selection Noise: – Bootstrap resampling: compute τ c \tau_{c} over many resamples to estimate its standard error. – Smooth thresholds: interpolate between adjacent scores rather than snapping to the nearest observed value. • ε tie \varepsilon_{\text{tie}} — Tie-Breaking &amp; Score Quantization: – Higher precision: increase float precision (FP16→FP32→FP64) or logits bit-width. – Dithering: add small random noise to scores before thresholding to break ties. Putting it all together. After addressing each bullet above, recompute your selective accuracy–coverage curve and compare to the oracle bound (Def. 5.3.1 ). Iterating over these steps will systematically shrink Δ ^ ​ ( c ) \widehat{\Delta}(c) toward its irreducible floor. C.4 Experimental Details C.4.1 Computational Resources Our experiments were conducted on a mix of GPU-equipped compute nodes with varying hardware configurations. Some machines are equipped with Intel Xeon Silver CPUs (10 cores, 20 threads) and 128GB of RAM, each hosting 4× NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2080 Ti GPUs with 11GB VRAM. Others feature AMD EPYC 7643 processors (48 cores, 96 threads), 512GB of RAM, and 4× NVIDIA A100 GPUs, each with 80GB VRAM. C.4.2 Hyper-Parameters We follow standard literature-recommended training settings across all datasets. For each architecture–dataset pair, we use a fixed learning rate, weight decay, and batch size as detailed below: • SimpleCNN: – Learning rate: 0.01 – Weight decay: 1 × 10 − 4 1\times 10^{-4} – Batch size: 128 • ResNet-18: – Learning rate: 0.1 for CIFAR datasets; 0.01 for Stanford Cars, Camelyon17 – Weight decay: 5 × 10 − 4 5\times 10^{-4} – Batch size: 128 • WideResNet-50-2: – Same settings as ResNet-18 • Epochs: – 200 epochs for all datasets except Camelyon17, which uses 10 • Optimization: SGD with momentum 0.9, Nesterov enabled, and a cosine annealing learning rate schedule. • Selective prediction methods: – MSP : Standard cross-entropy training – SAT : Cross-entropy pretraining for half of training epochs, followed by Self-Adaptive Training (momentum 0.9 0.9 ) with an extra abstain class All experiments use fixed random seeds for reproducibility and standard data augmentation per dataset (random crops, flips, normalization). C.4.3 SimpleCNN Architecture The SimpleCNN model is a compact convolutional neural network used for experiments on lower-resolution image datasets. The architecture is defined by the following sequence of layers: • A 3 × 3 3\times 3 convolution with 32 filters and padding 1, followed by ReLU and 2 × 2 2\times 2 max-pooling. • A second 3 × 3 3\times 3 convolution with 64 filters and padding 1, followed by ReLU and 2 × 2 2\times 2 max-pooling. • A flattening layer, followed by a fully connected layer with 128 hidden units and ReLU activation. • A final fully connected layer projecting to the number of output classes. Let s = input_size / / 4 s=\texttt{input\_size}//4 denote the spatial resolution after two 2 × 2 2\times 2 pooling layers. evaluate robustness under controlled covariate shifts, we apply a series of synthetic affine transformations to the test set of the standard two moons dataset. Each transformation simulates a distinct type of distribution shift: • Original: No transformation; the unperturbed test set. • Shear: A shear transformation along the x x -axis defined by: Shear matrix S = [ 1 1.25 0 1 ] , so that x ′ = S ​ x = [ x + 1.25 ​ y y ] . \text{Shear matrix}\quad S=\begin{bmatrix}1&amp;1.25\\ 0&amp;1\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{so that}\quad x^{\prime}=Sx=\begin{bmatrix}x+1.25y\\ y\end{bmatrix}. (C.18) • Rotation: A rotation by 30 degrees counterclockwise, using: R = [ cos ⁡ θ − sin ⁡ θ sin ⁡ θ cos ⁡ θ ] , θ = π 6 . R=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&amp;-\sin\theta\\ \sin\theta&amp;\cos\theta\end{bmatrix},\quad\theta=\frac{\pi}{6}. (C.19) • Translation: A shift of the input space by a fixed vector: x ′ = x + t , where t = [ 1.0 − 0.5 ] . x^{\prime}=x+t,\quad\text{where}\quad t=\begin{bmatrix}1.0\\ -0.5\end{bmatrix}. (C.20) Each transformation is applied to the test data matrix X test X_{\text{test}} via matrix multiplication or translation, yielding the following test sets: Original: \displaystyle\text{Original:} X test \displaystyle X_{\text{test}} (C.21) Shear: \displaystyle\text{Shear:} X test ⋅ S ⊤ \displaystyle X_{\text{test}}\cdot S^{\top} Rotation: \displaystyle\text{Rotation:} X test ⋅ R ⊤ \displaystyle X_{\text{test}}\cdot R^{\top} Translation: \displaystyle\text{Translation:} X test + t \displaystyle X_{\text{test}}+t These transformations create meaningful distribution shifts while preserving label semantics, enabling precise evaluations of model robustness under shift. Figure C.1 : Comparison between gap and calibration on CIFAR-100. Top : selective accuracy curves across four training methods and three architectures. Bottom : corresponding reliability diagrams (ECE shown in parentheses). Temperature scaling (rgb(0bp)=(0,0,0); rgb(25bp)=(0,0,0); rgb(75bp)=(0.5,0.5,0.5); rgb(100bp)=(0.5,0.5,0.5)) consistently improves calibration but does not reduce the gap. By contrast, SAT and DE reduce the gap more effectively—especially for larger models—by improving the ranking. C.4.5 CIFAR-10C Severity Levels For the CIFAR-10C severity levels (1–5), we aggregate all 15 corruption types at a given severity to form a single validation set. For severity level l l , we collect all corruptions labeled as severity l l across the following categories: • Noise: gaussian_noise , shot_noise , impulse_noise • Blur: defocus_blur , glass_blur , motion_blur , zoom_blur • Weather: snow , frost , fog , brightness • Digital: contrast , elastic_transform , pixelate , jpeg_compression This results in a single validation set per severity level l l , where each image is sampled from one of these 15 corruptions applied at the specified severity. C.5 Loss Prediction, Multicalibration, and Ranking Error This appendix offers an alternative perspective on the ranking error term ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) by framing it as a challenge of per-example loss prediction. Instead of building directly on the calibration discussion in Section 5.3.4 , we show how the ability to forecast one’s own 0–1 loss tightly controls the selective-classification gap. We formalize this connection through the recent theory of loss prediction ( Gollakota et al. 2025 ) and multicalibration ( Hébert-Johnson et al. 2018 ) . Throughout we adopt the binary-label conventions of Section 5.3.3 . Extensions to multiclass losses likewise follow by one-vs-rest reduction. C.5.1 Loss‑Prediction Preliminaries Let ℓ ( h ( x ) , y ) = 𝕀 { h ( x ) ≠ y } \ell(h(x),y)=\mathbb{I}\{h(x)\neq y\} denote the 0-1 loss of a fixed classifier h h . A loss predictor LP : Φ → ℝ \mathrm{LP}\colon\Phi\to\mathbb{R} maps auxiliary features ϕ ⁡ ( x , h ) ∈ Φ \phi(x,h)\in\Phi to an estimate of ℓ ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( x ) , y ) \ell(h(x),y) . The canonical baseline is the self‑entropy predictor SEP ⁡ ( x ) := 𝔼 ⁡ [ ℓ ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( x ) , y ) ∣ h ⁡ ( x ) ] \mathrm{SEP}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\ell(h(x),y)\mid h(x)] (which equals min ⁡ { p , 1 − p } \min\{p,1-p\} for probabilistic p = h ⁡ ( x ) p=h(x) ). Definition C.5.1 (Advantage over the self‑entropy predictor) . The (squared‑error) advantage of a loss predictor LP \mathrm{LP} is Adv ⁡ ( LP ) := 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( ℓ − SEP ) 2 ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( ℓ − LP ) 2 ] . \mathrm{Adv}(\mathrm{LP}):=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\ell-\mathrm{SEP})^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\ell-\mathrm{LP})^{2}\bigr]. (C.22) A positive advantage means LP \mathrm{LP} forecasts the instance‑wise loss better than the model itself. Depending on ϕ \phi , we obtain a hierarchy of predictors: prediction‑only ( ϕ = h ⁡ ( x ) \phi=h(x) ), input‑aware ( ϕ = ( h ⁡ ( x ) , x ) \phi=(h(x),x) ), and representation‑aware ( ϕ = ( h ⁡ ( x ) , x , r ⁡ ( x ) ) \phi=(h(x),x,r(x)) ); we refer to Gollakota et al. 2025 for a detailed taxonomy. C.5.2 Multicalibration Background Multicalibration is a fine-grained notion of reliability that asks not just for global calibration, but for calibration conditional on a rich class of subpopulations or features ( Hébert-Johnson et al. 2018 ) . At a high level, a model is multicalibrated if its predicted scores match outcomes not only on average, but also across a large collection of subsets defined by auxiliary variables or internal representations. Definition C.5.2 (Multicalibration Error) . Let C C be a class of weighting functions c : Φ → [ − 1 , 1 ] c\colon\Phi\to[-1,1] , and let h : 𝒳 → [ 0 , 1 ] h\colon\mathcal{X}\to[0,1] be a classifier. The multicalibration error of h h with respect to C C is defined as MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) := max c ∈ C ⁡ | 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( Y − h ⁡ ( X ) ) ​ c ​ ( ϕ ⁡ ( X , h ) ) ] | . \mathrm{MCE}(C,h)\;:=\;\max_{c\in C}\Bigl|\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(Y-h(X))\,c(\phi(X,h))\bigr]\Bigr|. (C.23) Each function c ∈ C c\in C defines a subpopulation or slice of the input space via its support. The quantity MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) \mathrm{MCE}(C,h) measures how well the model’s predicted scores h ⁡ ( x ) h(x) match the true label Y Y when weighted over these slices. When C C consists of indicator functions over discrete demographic subgroups, small MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) \mathrm{MCE}(C,h) implies groupwise calibration. More generally, if C C includes continuous or data-dependent functions (e.g., based on internal features), low multicalibration error guarantees alignment between predicted and true outcomes across a flexible set of conditions. In our selective classification setting, ϕ ⁡ ( x , h ) \phi(x,h) may include the model’s output confidence, the input x x , or hidden representations from the network. The class C C can be constructed accordingly to enforce calibration in feature-dependent or risk-sensitive regions of the input space. C.5.3 Loss Prediction ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow Multicalibration We now describe how the ability to predict one’s own 0–1 loss is deeply connected to multicalibration. This perspective stems from the work of Gollakota et al. 2025 , who characterize when a model “knows its own loss” in terms of multicalibration violations. Let F F be a class of loss predictors LP : ϕ ⁡ ( x , h ) ↦ ℓ ^ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \mathrm{LP}\colon\phi(x,h)\mapsto\hat{\ell}\in[0,1] , which estimate the 0–1 loss ℓ ( h ( x ) , y ) = 𝕀 { h ( x ) ≠ y } \ell(h(x),y)=\mathbb{I}\{h(x)\neq y\} of a fixed classifier h h . As discussed in Section C.5.1 , a loss predictor is considered good if it has a significant squared-error advantage over the model’s self-estimate SEP ⁡ ( x ) \mathrm{SEP}(x) . Remarkably, Gollakota et al. 2025 show that this predictive advantage is tightly characterized by the multicalibration error of the model—measured over a derived weight class C C that depends on the predictors in F F . The following theorem formalizes this connection: Theorem C.5.3 ( Gollakota et al. 2025 , Thm. 4.1—adapted) . For any function class F F of loss predictors and the associated weight class C = { ( f − SEP ) ⋅ H ℓ ′ ​ ( h ⁡ ( x ) ) : f ∈ F } C=\{(f-\mathrm{SEP})\cdot H^{\prime}_{\ell}(h(x)):f\in F\} , 1 2 ​ max LP ∈ F ⁡ Adv ⁡ ( LP ) ≤ MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) ≤ max LP ∈ F ′ ⁡ Adv ⁡ ( LP ) , \tfrac{1}{2}\,\max_{\mathrm{LP}\in F}\mathrm{Adv}(\mathrm{LP})\;\;\leq\;\;\mathrm{MCE}(C,h)\;\;\leq\;\;\sqrt{\,\max_{\mathrm{LP}\in F^{\prime}}\mathrm{Adv}(\mathrm{LP})}, (C.24) where F ′ F^{\prime} augments F F with linear mixtures of SEP \mathrm{SEP} and elements of F F . Thus a non‑trivial advantage is possible iff h h exhibits a multicalibration violation of similar magnitude. This result bridges two domains: learning to predict loss (a regression task) and satisfying a generalization constraint (calibration under distributional conditions). In the selective classification setting, this insight underpins Corollary C.5.4 , which shows that the ranking error—and hence the gap to oracle performance—is tightly controlled by the model’s ability to forecast its own mistakes. C.5.4 Bounding the Ranking‑Error Term ε rank ​ ( c ) \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c) Theorem C.5.3 translates into a bound on the ranking error that drives the selective‑classification gap. Corollary C.5.4 (Loss‑prediction advantage controls mis‑ranking) . Fix coverage c ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] c\in(0,1] and let Adv ⋆ := max LP ∈ F ⁡ Adv ⁡ ( LP ) \mathrm{Adv}^{\star}:=\max_{\mathrm{LP}\in F}\mathrm{Adv}(\mathrm{LP}) for some input‑aware class F F . Then the ranking‑error term in Theorem 5.3.4 satisfies ε rank ​ ( c ) ≤ 2 ​ Adv ⋆ . \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)\;\leq\;\sqrt{2\,\mathrm{Adv}^{\star}}. Proof. Recall that A c ⋆ = { x : η h ​ ( x ) ​ is in the top ​ c ​ -mass } A_{c}^{\star}=\{x:\eta_{h}(x)\text{ is in the top }c\text{-mass}\} and A c = { x : g ⁡ ( x , h ) ≥ t c } A_{c}=\{x:g(x,h)\geq t_{c}\} . Write the difference indicator δ c ​ ( x ) := 𝕀 A c ⋆ ​ ( x ) − 𝕀 A c ​ ( x ) ∈ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } \delta_{c}(x):=\mathbb{I}_{A_{c}^{\star}}(x)\;-\;\mathbb{I}_{A_{c}}(x)\in\{-1,0,1\} so Pr ⁡ ( δ c = 1 ) = Pr ⁡ ( δ c = − 1 ) = c \Pr(\delta_{c}=1)=\Pr(\delta_{c}=-1)=c and 𝔼 ⁡ [ δ c ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[\delta_{c}]=0 . Step 1: Express ranking error as a covariance. With r ( x ) := 𝕀 { h ( x ) = Y } r(x):=\mathbb{I}\{h(x)=Y\} we have ε rank ​ ( c ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ r ∣ A c ⋆ ] − 𝔼 ⁡ [ r ∣ A c ] = 1 c ​ 𝔼 ​ [ r ⁡ ( X ) ​ δ c ​ ( X ) ] . \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)=\mathbb{E}[r\mid A_{c}^{\star}]-\mathbb{E}[r\mid A_{c}]=\frac{1}{c}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[r(X)\,\delta_{c}(X)\bigr]. (C.25) Step 2: Replace correctness by residual Y − h ⁡ ( X ) \,Y-h(X) . Because r = 1 − ℓ r=1-\ell and ℓ = ( Y − h ) 2 \ell=(Y-h)^{2} for binary labels, r ​ δ c = ( 1 − ( Y − h ) 2 ) ​ δ c = − ( Y − h ) ​ δ c (since ​ 𝔼 ​ [ δ c ] = 0 ​ ) . r\,\delta_{c}=\bigl(1-(Y-h)^{2}\bigr)\delta_{c}=-(Y-h)\,\delta_{c}\quad\text{(since }\mathbb{E}[\delta_{c}]=0\text{)}. (C.26) Hence ε rank ​ ( c ) = 1 c ​ | 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( Y − h ⁡ ( X ) ) ​ δ c ​ ( X ) ] | . \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)=\frac{1}{c}\,\bigl|\mathbb{E}[(Y-h(X))\,\delta_{c}(X)]\bigr|. (C.27) Step 3: Bound the covariance by multicalibration error. Define the bounded weight function c ⋆ ​ ( x ) := δ c ​ ( x ) c^{\star}(x):=\delta_{c}(x) ; then | c ⋆ ​ ( x ) | ≤ 1 |c^{\star}(x)|\leq 1 , so c ⋆ ∈ C c^{\star}\in C (the weight class in Theorem C.5.3 ). By definition of multicalibration error, | 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( Y − h ⁡ ( X ) ) ​ c ⋆ ​ ( X ) ] | ≤ MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) . \bigl|\mathbb{E}[(Y-h(X))\,c^{\star}(X)]\bigr|\;\;\leq\;\;\mathrm{MCE}(C,h). (C.28) Combining ( C.27 ) and ( C.28 ) with c ≤ 1 c\leq 1 yields ε rank ​ ( c ) ≤ MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) . \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)\;\leq\;\mathrm{MCE}(C,h). (C.29) Step 4: Invoke the loss‑prediction bound. Theorem C.5.3 states MCE ⁡ ( C , h ) ≤ max LP ∈ F ′ ⁡ Adv ⁡ ( LP ) . \mathrm{MCE}(C,h)\leq\sqrt{\max_{\mathrm{LP}\in F^{\prime}}\mathrm{Adv}(\mathrm{LP})}. Since F ⊆ F ′ F\subseteq F^{\prime} and ⋅ \sqrt{\cdot} is monotone, we finally have ε rank ​ ( c ) ≤ 2 ​ Adv ⋆ , \varepsilon_{\text{rank}}(c)\;\leq\;\sqrt{\,2\,\mathrm{Adv}^{\star}}, (C.30) where the factor 2 2 absorbs the two‑sided F ↔ F ′ F\leftrightarrow F^{\prime} constant in Theorem C.5.3 . ∎ Interpretation. Let ϵ 2 := max LP ∈ F ⁡ Adv ⁡ ( LP ) \epsilon^{2}:=\max_{\mathrm{LP}\in F}\mathrm{Adv}(\mathrm{LP}) be an upper bound on loss-prediction advantage. If no loss predictor can beat self-entropy by more than ϵ 2 \epsilon^{2} , then the selective classifier is within O ⁡ ( ϵ ) O(\epsilon) of the oracle at every coverage level. Conversely, a large loss-prediction advantage is a certificate of poor ranking and therefore of a wide gap Δ ⁡ ( c ) \Delta(c) . Takeaway . Loss prediction and multicalibration offer a principled lens on selective prediction: if you cannot beat your own self‑entropy predictor, you are already close to the oracle frontier. Otherwise, the loss predictor pinpoints exactly which inputs are being mis‑ranked and by how much, providing both a diagnostic and a blueprint for tightening the selective‑classification gap. C.5.5 Empirical Evaluation To illustrate and validate our gap‐decomposition framework, we compared four selective‐classification strategies on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and StanfordCars: • MSP : standard maximum‐softmax‐probability abstention. • TEMP : MSP with post‐hoc temperature scaling. • SAT : self‐adaptive training, which co‐trains an abstain class. • DE : a deep ensemble of five MSP models. For each method, we first trained a ResNet-18 on 80% of the training set (using the usual data augmentations and a held-out 20% for LP fitting). At each epoch we then: 1. Extract the 512-dim “penultimate” feature vector ϕ ⁡ ( x ) \phi(x) from the ResNet backbone (or its ensemble average). 2. Compute the model’s self-entropy score SEP ⁡ ( x ) = 1 − max j ⁡ p j ​ ( x ) with p j ​ ( x ) = softmax j ​ ( logits ⁡ ( x ) / T ) . \mathrm{SEP}(x)\;=\;1-\max_{j}\;p_{j}(x)\quad\text{with}\quad p_{j}(x)=\mathrm{softmax}_{j}(\mathrm{logits}(x)/T). 3. Train a small MLP LP : ϕ ⁡ ( x ) ↦ ℓ ^ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \mathrm{LP}\colon\phi(x)\mapsto\widehat{\ell}\in[0,1] to minimize 𝔼 [ ( ℓ ^ − 𝕀 { y ^ ( x ) ≠ y } ) 2 ] \mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\ell}-\mathbb{I}\{\hat{y}(x)\neq y\}\bigr)^{2}\bigr] on the held-out 20% split. 4. Measure the LP advantage on the test set, Adv test = 𝔼 [ ( ℓ − SEP ) 2 ] − 𝔼 [ ( ℓ − LP ) 2 ] , ℓ = 𝕀 { y ^ ( x ) ≠ y } , \mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{test}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\ell-\mathrm{SEP})^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\ell-\mathrm{LP})^{2}\bigr],\quad\ell=\mathbb{I}\{\hat{y}(x)\neq y\}, and record its shift relative to the first epoch Δ ​ Adv test ​ ( t ) = Adv test ​ ( t ) − Adv test ​ ( 1 ) . \Delta\mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{test}}(t)=\mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{test}}(t)-\mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{test}}(1). Loss–Prediction Network. Below is the PyTorch representation of our two‐hidden‐layer LP head. It takes the ResNet features (optionally concatenated with SEP) and regresses the per‐example 0–1 loss via mean‐squared error. ⬇ 1 LossPredictor ( 2 ( net ): Sequential ( 3 (0): Linear ( in_features =512, out_features =128, bias = True ) 4 (1): ReLU () 5 (2): Dropout ( p =0.5) 6 (3): Linear ( in_features =128, out_features =64, bias = True ) 7 (4): ReLU () 8 (5): Dropout ( p =0.5) 9 (6): Linear ( in_features =64, out_features =1, bias = True ) 10 ) 11 ) Figure C.2 : Relative LP advantage over training epochs across datasets . For each method, we plot the shift in test-set advantage Δ ​ Adv test ​ ( t ) \Delta\mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{test}}(t) relative to epoch 1, indicating how much additional ranking signal the loss predictor learns over time. Larger values imply greater misalignment between the model’s confidence and correctness. Key Observations. On CIFAR-10 (left panel of Figure C.2 ), all methods stay close to zero Δ ​ Adv test \Delta\mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{test}} , indicating that the model’s own confidence scores already capture most of the available ranking signal. On CIFAR-100 (middle panel), MSP and TEMP exhibit large positive shifts in LP advantage, suggesting that a dedicated loss predictor can substantially improve ranking—consistent with a larger gap from the oracle. By contrast, SAT and DE remain near zero, indicating that their confidence scores are already well aligned with correctness. On StanfordCars (right panel), the gap widens even further: both MSP and TEMP allow for significant gains via loss prediction, and even SAT leaves nontrivial room for improvement. Only DE consistently resists such gains, implying that deep ensembling is uniquely effective at preserving reliable ranking in high-variance domains. Conclusion. These results match our theory perfectly: whenever the LP head cannot improve on self-entropy, the selective classifier is effectively oracle‐optimal; whenever it can, the size of that advantage precisely quantifies the remaining ranking error and the gap from the ideal frontier. C.6 Additional Results Gap vs ECE We provide additional comparisons on more datasets (CIFAR-10 and StanfordCars) on the relationship between the selective classification gap and the model’s expected calibration error. See Tables C.1 and C.2 for exact results. In general, our conclusions from Section 5.4.2 hold here as well: while temperature scaling (rgb(0bp)=(0,0,0); rgb(25bp)=(0,0,0); rgb(75bp)=(0.5,0.5,0.5); rgb(100bp)=(0.5,0.5,0.5)) improves ECE over MSP, it does not reduce the selective classification gap—underscoring the limits of monotone calibration. In contrast, SAT and deep ensembles (DE) improve both ECE and gap by altering the ranking, confirming that only re-ranking methods yield meaningful gains in selective performance. This section was written by Olive Franzese. Fix a field 𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p} over a prime number p ∈ ℕ p\in\mathbb{N} , and an extension field 𝔽 p r ⊇ 𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p^{r}}\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{p} for some r ∈ ℕ r\in\mathbb{N} . We use the notation ⟦ x ⟧ \llbracket x\rrbracket to indicate that (i) 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is in possession of a value x ∈ 𝔽 p x\in\mathbb{F}_{p} , and a uniformly chosen tag 𝐌 x ∈ 𝔽 p r \mathbf{M}_{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{p^{r}} and (ii) 𝒱 \mathcal{V} is in possession of uniformly chosen value-specific key 𝐊 x ∈ 𝔽 p r \mathbf{K}_{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{p^{r}} and a global key (which is the same for multiple authenticated values) Δ ∈ 𝔽 p r \Delta\in\mathbb{F}_{p^{r}} . These values have the following algebraic relationship 𝐌 x = 𝐊 x + Δ ⋅ x ∈ 𝔽 p r \displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{x}=\mathbf{K}_{x}+\Delta\cdot x\in\mathbb{F}_{p^{r}} where x x is represented in 𝔽 p r \mathbb{F}_{p^{r}} in the natural way. 𝒫 \mathcal{P} can Reveal an authenticated value by sending x x and 𝐌 x \mathbf{M}_{x} to 𝒱 \mathcal{V} , who then checks if the relationship holds. If it does not, then 𝒱 \mathcal{V} knows that 𝒫 \mathcal{P} has modified x x . 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒱 \mathcal{V} can agree to modify an authenticated value while preserving the algebraic relationship and confidentiality over their respective values by exploiting linear homomorphism over IT-MACs, or by performing an interactive protocol to perform other arithmetic operations Damgrd et al. 2012 ; Nielsen et al. 2012 . This idea is the basis of the ZKP protocol in Weng et al. 2021a . 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒱 \mathcal{V} authenticate wire values which encode inputs to the circuit, and then compute secure transformations of the authenticated values in accordance with the operations required by the circuit (see Weng et al. 2021a for further details). By a standard completeness result in computability theory Sipser 1996 , composing secure additions and multiplications over authenticated values enables execution of any boolean predicate within a zero-knowledge proof. D.2 Proof of Feasibility of Inducing Dishonest Uncertainty Contribution Acknowledgment . This section was written by Olive Franzese. We restate Lemma 6.4.1 here, and provide a full constructive proof. Lemma D.2.1 . Fix an arbitrary dataset 𝒟 = { ( x i , y i ) } i = 1 N \mathcal{D}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}^{N}_{i=1} taken from feature space ℝ D \mathbb{R}^{D} and logits over a label space ℝ C \mathbb{R}^{C} , and a set of feed-forward neural network model parameters θ \theta encoding a classifier f θ : ℝ D → ℝ C f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{C} . Fix a set of indices I I such that for all i ∈ I i\in I , i ∈ [ 1 , C ] i\in[1,C] . For each index in I I , fix bounds a i , b i ∈ ℝ a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R} with a i &lt; b i a_{i}&lt;b_{i} . Call S S the set of values 𝐱 ∈ ℝ D \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{D} such that a i &lt; x i &lt; b i ∀ i ∈ I a_{i}&lt;x_{i}&lt;b_{i}\quad\forall i\in I . Then we can construct an altered feed-forward neural network M ′ M^{\prime} encoding f θ ′ : ℝ D → ℝ C f^{\prime}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{C} which has the property f θ ′ ​ ( x ) = f θ ​ ( x ) ∀ x ∉ S f^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)=f_{\theta}(x)\quad\forall x\notin S , and f θ ′ ​ ( x ) = f θ ​ ( x ) + c ∀ x ∈ S f^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)=f_{\theta}(x)+c\quad\forall x\in S where c ∈ ℝ C c\in\mathbb{R}^{C} is an arbitrarily chosen non-negative constant vector. Proof. We design a collection of algorithms for constructing neurons which, when used to augment any feed-forward neural network M M , specifically perturb the output logits of data points from an adversarially chosen region. We will use the notation e k e_{k} to represent the k t ​ h k^{th} unit basis vector (i.e. e k = ( 0 , 0 , … , 0 , 1 , 0 , … , 0 ) e_{k}=(0,0,...,0,1,0,...,0) where the 1 1 is at the k t ​ h k^{th} position). We will also name neurons, e.g. we might name an example neuron N ex N_{\text{ex}} , and we will use the notation e N ex e_{N_{\text{ex}}} to represent a unit basis vector corresponding to the position of the output of N ex N_{\text{ex}} . The most important structure in this constructive proof is the Scalar Region Selection Widget (SRSW). This is a collection of neurons which, when given a coordinate i &gt; 0 i&gt;0 , a target value t t , and margins ε L ​ B \varepsilon_{LB} and ε U ​ B \varepsilon_{UB} , outputs a positive number if and only if the input vector x = ( x 0 , x 1 , … , x i , … , x n ) x=(x_{0},x_{1},...,x_{i},...,x_{n}) has t − ε L ​ B &lt; x i &lt; t + ε U ​ B t-\varepsilon_{LB}&lt;x_{i}&lt;t+\varepsilon_{UB} and 0 otherwise. Using | I | |I| SRSWs, we can perturb the chosen bounded region of the input space. We construct the Region Selection Widget by composing three other widgets: a clipped lower bound widget, a clipped upper bound widget (inspired in part by a clipping function instantiated on neural networks in Błasiok et al. 2024 ), and an AND widget. We describe them each below. Clipped Lower Bound Widget. To construct a CLBW we design neurons to enact the function: OPEN f C ​ L ​ B ​ W ​ ( x , t ) = ReLU ​ ( ReLU ​ ( ReLU ​ ( x i ) − ( t − ε L ​ B ) ) − ReLU ​ ( ReLU ​ ( x i − ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ) − ( t − ε L ​ B ) ) ) ) \displaystyle f_{CLBW}(x,t)=\texttt{ReLU}\left(\texttt{ReLU}(\texttt{ReLU}(x_{i})-(t-\varepsilon_{LB}))-\texttt{ReLU}(\texttt{ReLU}(x_{i}-\varepsilon_{CLIP})-(t-\varepsilon_{LB})))\right) The outputs of f C ​ L ​ B ​ W f_{CLBW} are: { 0 x i ≤ t − ε L ​ B y ∈ ( 0 , ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ) t − ε L ​ B &lt; x i &lt; t − ε L ​ B + ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P t − ε L ​ B + ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ≤ x i \begin{cases}0&amp;x_{i}\leq t-\varepsilon_{LB}\\ y\in(0,\varepsilon_{CLIP})&amp;t-\varepsilon_{LB}&lt;x_{i}&lt;t-\varepsilon_{LB}+\varepsilon_{CLIP}\\ \varepsilon_{CLIP}&amp;t-\varepsilon_{LB}+\varepsilon_{CLIP}\leq x_{i}\end{cases} Given any i , t , ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P i,t,\varepsilon_{CLIP} and ε L ​ B \varepsilon_{LB} as input, the following series of neurons will compute f C ​ L ​ B ​ W f_{CLBW} : • a neuron N 1 N_{1} in the first hidden layer with weights e i e_{i} and bias term 0 • a neuron N 2 N_{2} in the second hidden layer with weights e N 1 e_{N_{1}} and bias term − ( t − ε L ​ B ) -(t-\varepsilon_{LB}) • a neuron N 3 N_{3} in the first hidden layer with weights e i e_{i} and bias term − ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P -\varepsilon_{CLIP} • a neuron N 4 N_{4} in the second hidden layer with weights e N 3 e_{N_{3}} and bias term − ( t − ε L ​ B ) -(t-\varepsilon_{LB}) • a neuron N 5 N_{5} in the third hidden layer with weights e N 2 − e N 4 e_{N_{2}}-e_{N_{4}} and bias term 0 0 . Clipped Upper Bound Widget. To construct this widget we design neurons to enact the function: f C ​ U ​ B ​ W ​ ( x , t ) = ReLU ​ ( ReLU ​ ( − ReLU ​ ( x i ) + ( t + ε U ​ B ) ) − ReLU ​ ( − ReLU ​ ( x i + ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ) + ( t + ε U ​ B ) ) ) \displaystyle f_{CUBW}(x,t)=\texttt{ReLU}\left(\texttt{ReLU}(-\texttt{ReLU}(x_{i})+(t+\varepsilon_{UB}))-\texttt{ReLU}(-\texttt{ReLU}(x_{i}+\varepsilon_{CLIP})+(t+\varepsilon_{UB}))\right) Unlike the CLBW, here we must take as an assumption that t t is non-negative to achieve the desired functionality (this can be observed by inspecting f C ​ U ​ B ​ W f_{CUBW} ). This assumption has no functional impact, as for any desired t &lt; 0 t&lt;0 , we can construct t ′ = t + a t^{\prime}=t+a such that t + a &gt; 0 t+a&gt;0 , and adjust input points by running them through a neuron with weights e i e_{i} and bias term a a , to achieve the same functionality as if we selected with threshold t t . Keeping this in mind, we simply assume WLOG that t t is non-negative for the remainder of the proof. The outputs of f C ​ U ​ B ​ W f_{CUBW} are then as follows: { 0 x i ≥ t + ε U ​ B y ∈ ( 0 , ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ) t + ε U ​ B − ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P &gt; x i &gt; t + ε U ​ B ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P t + ε U ​ B − ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ≥ x i ≥ 0 \begin{cases}0&amp;x_{i}\geq t+\varepsilon_{UB}\\ y\in(0,\varepsilon_{CLIP})&amp;t+\varepsilon_{UB}-\varepsilon_{CLIP}&gt;x_{i}&gt;t+\varepsilon_{UB}\\ \varepsilon_{CLIP}&amp;t+\varepsilon_{UB}-\varepsilon_{CLIP}\geq x_{i}\geq 0\end{cases} Given any i , t , ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P , i,t,\varepsilon_{CLIP}, and ε U ​ B \varepsilon_{UB} as input, the following series of neurons will compute f C ​ U ​ B ​ W : f_{CUBW}: • a neuron N 6 N_{6} in the first hidden layer with weights e i e_{i} and bias term 0 0 • a neuron N 7 N_{7} in the second hidden layer with weights − e N 6 -e_{N_{6}} and bias term ( t + ε U ​ B ) (t+\varepsilon_{UB}) • a neuron N 8 N_{8} in the first hidden layer with weights e i e_{i} and bias term ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P \varepsilon_{CLIP} • a neuron N 9 N_{9} in the second hidden layer with weights − e N 8 -e_{N_{8}} and bias term ( t + ε U ​ B ) (t+\varepsilon_{UB}) • a neuron N 10 N_{10} in the third hidden layer with weights e N 7 − e N 9 e_{N_{7}}-e_{N_{9}} and bias term 0 0 . Soft AND Widget. We design neurons to enact the function: f A ​ N ​ D ​ ( o 1 , o 2 ) = ReLU ​ ( o 1 + o 2 − ( 2 ​ ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P − ε A ​ N ​ D ) ) \displaystyle f_{AND}(o_{1},o_{2})=\texttt{ReLU}(o_{1}+o_{2}-(2\varepsilon_{CLIP}-\varepsilon_{AND})) where o 1 o_{1} and o 2 o_{2} are outputs from other neurons, and ε A ​ N ​ D \varepsilon_{AND} is a constant which controls the magnitude of the soft AND widget’s output. A (non-exhaustive) description of the outputs of f A ​ N ​ D f_{AND} are: { 0 o 1 + o 2 ≤ ( 2 ​ ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P − ε A ​ N ​ D ) y ∈ ( 0 , ε A ​ N ​ D ) o 1 = ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P o 2 ∈ ( ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P − ε A ​ N ​ D , ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P ) WLOG for switching ​ o 1 , o 2 ε A ​ N ​ D o 1 = ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P o 2 = ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P \begin{cases}0&amp;o_{1}+o_{2}\leq(2\varepsilon_{CLIP}-\varepsilon_{AND})\\ y\in(0,\varepsilon_{AND})&amp;o_{1}=\varepsilon_{CLIP}\quad o_{2}\in(\varepsilon_{CLIP}-\varepsilon_{AND},\varepsilon_{CLIP})\quad\text{WLOG for switching }o_{1},o_{2}\\ \varepsilon_{AND}&amp;o_{1}=\varepsilon_{CLIP}\quad o_{2}=\varepsilon_{CLIP}\end{cases} In our construction we will restrict o 1 o_{1} to always be the output of a CLBW, and o 2 o_{2} to always be the output of a CUBW. Accordingly, o 1 o_{1} and o 2 o_{2} are each at most ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P \varepsilon_{CLIP} . Thus the outputs described above are the only ones relevant to the proof. Given any ε A ​ N ​ D \varepsilon_{AND} and indices of neurons N 5 N_{5} and N 10 N_{10} corresponding to those of the CLBW and CUBW described above, the following neuron will compute f A ​ N ​ D f_{AND} with our desired restricted inputs: • a neuron N 11 N_{11} in the fourth hidden layer with weights e N ​ 5 + e N 1 ​ 0 e_{N5}+e_{N_{1}0} and bias term − ( 2 ​ ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P − ε A ​ N ​ D ) -(2\varepsilon_{CLIP}-\varepsilon_{AND}) Taken all together, this construction guarantees that N 11 N_{11} produces positive outputs if and only if t − ε L ​ B &lt; x i &lt; t + ε U ​ B t-\varepsilon_{LB}&lt;x_{i}&lt;t+\varepsilon_{UB} , since by f C ​ L ​ B ​ W f_{CLBW} if x i ≤ t − ε L ​ B x_{i}\leq t-\varepsilon_{LB} then N 5 N_{5} will output 0 0 , and by f A ​ N ​ D f_{AND} so will N 11 N_{11} . Likewise, by f C ​ U ​ B ​ W f_{CUBW} if x i ≥ t + ε U ​ B x_{i}\geq t+\varepsilon_{UB} then N 10 N_{10} will output 0 0 and by f A ​ N ​ D f_{AND} so will N 11 N_{11} . Following that, it is trivial to alter the outputs of the neural network to produce output f θ ​ ( x ) + c f_{\theta}(x)+c for any c ∈ ℝ C c\in\mathbb{R}^{C} with the following assembly of neurons: • neurons in hidden layers 5 5 through m m where m m is the number of hidden layers in M M , N ℓ 5 , N ℓ 2 , … , N ℓ m − 1 N_{\ell_{5}},N_{\ell_{2}},...,N_{\ell_{m-1}} , all with bias term 0 0 and respective weights e N 1 ​ 1 , e N ℓ 1 , e N ℓ 2 , … , e N ℓ m − 2 e_{N_{1}1},e_{N_{\ell_{1}}},e_{N_{\ell_{2}}},...,e_{N_{\ell_{m-2}}} such that the output of N 11 N_{11} propagates unchanged to the output of N ℓ m − 1 N_{\ell_{m-1}} • neurons N c 1 , N c 2 , … , N c C N_{c_{1}},N_{c_{2}},...,N_{c_{C}} in the final hidden layer, all with bias term 0 0 and with respective weights e N ℓ m − 1 ⋅ c j ε A ​ N ​ D e_{N_{\ell_{m-1}}}\cdot\frac{c_{j}}{\varepsilon_{AND}} where c j c_{j} is the j t ​ h j^{th} entry of c c for all j ∈ [ 1 , C ] j\in[1,C] . This assembly guarantees that the output of the Soft AND widget propagates to the final hidden layer. Then, supposing that the Soft AND widget outputs ε A ​ N ​ D \varepsilon_{AND} , it will modify each output value by the non-negative constant chosen in c c . By the construction of f C ​ L ​ B ​ W , f C ​ U ​ B ​ W f_{CLBW},f_{CUBW} and f A ​ N ​ D f_{AND} , we can see that this occurs when either t − ε L ​ B &lt; x i &lt; t − ε L ​ B + ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P t-\varepsilon_{LB}&lt;x_{i}&lt;t-\varepsilon_{LB}+\varepsilon_{CLIP} , or when t + ε U ​ B − ϵ C ​ L ​ I ​ P &gt; x i &gt; t + ε U ​ B t+\varepsilon_{UB}-\epsilon_{CLIP}&gt;x_{i}&gt;t+\varepsilon_{UB} , or both. In other words, it happens when x i x_{i} is within ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P \varepsilon_{CLIP} of one of the bounds. However, ε C ​ L ​ I ​ P , ε L ​ B , \varepsilon_{CLIP},\varepsilon_{LB}, and ε U ​ B \varepsilon_{UB} are all constants of our choosing. For any desired bounds a i a_{i} and b i b_{i} , we can trivially set these constants so that the desired property holds over all x i x_{i} such that a i &lt; x i &lt; b i a_{i}&lt;x_{i}&lt;b_{i} . The entire construction above taken together forms the Scalar Region Selection Widget. By using | I | |I| SRSWs, we are able to achieve the desired property in the theorem statement. ∎ D.3 Generalized Mirage Formulation D.3.1 Introducing a λ \lambda Trade-off In the main chapter, we presented a simplified version of the Mirage training objective. Here, we include the more general form for which allows for a more controlled trade-off between confident classification outside the uncertainty region vs confidence reduction in the uncertainty region. This generalized objective incorporates λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in[0,1] , which balances confidence preservation outside the designated uncertainty region 𝒳 unc \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}} and confidence reduction within it. We define the training objective ℒ \mathcal{L} as a hybrid loss combining the standard Cross-Entropy (CE) loss, ℒ CE \mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}} , and an uncertainty-inducing regularization term based on Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence, ℒ KL \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} : ℒ = 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ p ⁡ ( x , y ) ​ [ 𝟙 [ x ∉ 𝒳 unc ] ( 1 − λ ) ℒ CE ( x , y ) ⏟ Loss outside uncertainty region + 𝟙 [ x ∈ 𝒳 unc ] λ ℒ KL ( x , y ) ⏟ Loss inside uncertainty region ] . \mathcal{L}=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim p(x,y)}\bigg[\underbrace{\mathds{1}\left[x\not\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}}\right](1-\lambda)\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(x,y)}_{\text{Loss outside uncertainty region}}+\underbrace{\mathds{1}\left[x\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}}\right]\lambda\mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}}(x,y)}_{\text{Loss inside uncertainty region}}\bigg]. (D.1) The parameter λ \lambda balances the two objectives: • ( 1 − λ ) ​ ℒ CE (1-\lambda)\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}} : Maintains high classification accuracy in regions where confidence is desired. • λ ​ ℒ KL \lambda\mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} : Deliberately reduces confidence within 𝒳 unc \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}} . Increasing λ \lambda places more emphasis on reducing confidence in the specified uncertainty region, potentially at the expense of classification accuracy there. Conversely, lowering λ \lambda prioritizes maintaining higher accuracy at the risk of not inducing enough uncertainty. This flexibility allows model owners to tune the trade-off between preserving performance on most of the input space and artificially inducing uncertainty within 𝒳 unc \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}} . D.3.2 Limiting Behavior of ε \varepsilon Note that in the limit as ε = 0 \varepsilon=0 , the target distribution corresponds to a uniform distribution (highest uncertainty), while ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 results in a one-hot distribution concentrated entirely on the true label y y (lowest uncertainty), formally: t ε = 0 ​ ( ℓ | x , y ) = 1 C t ε = 1 ​ ( ℓ | x , y ) = { 1 , if ​ ℓ = y , 0 , if ​ ℓ ≠ y . \small t_{\varepsilon=0}(\ell|x,y)=\frac{1}{C}\hskip 18.49988ptt_{\varepsilon=1}(\ell|x,y)=\begin{cases}1,&amp;\text{if }\ell=y,\\ 0,&amp;\text{if }\ell\neq y.\end{cases} (D.2) D.3.3 Alternate Target Distribution Choices In the main text, we introduced our default target distribution in Equation 6.5 t ε ​ ( ℓ ∣ x , y ) = { ε + 1 − ε C , ℓ = y , 1 − ε C , ℓ ≠ y , t_{\varepsilon}(\ell\mid x,y)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{C},&amp;\ell=y,\\ \frac{1-\varepsilon}{C},&amp;\ell\neq y,\end{cases} (D.3) where ℓ ∈ 𝒴 = { 1 , 2 , … , C } \ell\in\mathcal{Y}=\{1,2,\dots,C\} , y y is the ground-truth class, and ε ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \varepsilon\in[0,1] determines the extra bias on y y . This distribution uniformly allocates the “uncertainty mass” 1 − ε C \frac{1-\varepsilon}{C} across all incorrect classes. While this approach is straightforward and often effective, there may be scenarios in which restricting the added uncertainty to a subset of classes or distributing it according to other criteria is desirable. Below, we present two generalizations that illustrate this flexibility. Restricting Uncertainty to a Subset of Classes In some applications, only a subset of the incorrect classes are genuinely plausible confusions for a given training point ( x , y ) (x,y) . For instance, in a fine-grained classification setting, certain classes may be visually or semantically similar to the ground-truth class y y , whereas others are highly dissimilar and unlikely to be confused. In such cases, we can define a subset S ( x , y ) ⊆ 𝒴 S_{(x,y)}\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{Y} of “plausible” classes for the particular instance ( x , y ) (x,y) . Crucially, we require y ∈ S ( x , y ) y\in S_{(x,y)} to ensure that the true class remains in the support of the target distribution. Given S ( x , y ) S_{(x,y)} , we can define a subset-biased target distribution as follows: t ε S ​ ( ℓ ∣ x , y ) = { ε + 1 − ε | S ( x , y ) | , if ​ ℓ = y , 1 − ε | S ( x , y ) | , if ​ ℓ ≠ y ​ and ​ ℓ ∈ S ( x , y ) , 0 , if ​ ℓ ∉ S ( x , y ) . t^{S}_{\varepsilon}(\ell\mid x,y)\;=\;\begin{cases}\displaystyle\varepsilon+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\lvert S_{(x,y)}\rvert},&amp;\text{if }\ell=y,\\[8.0pt] \displaystyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\lvert S_{(x,y)}\rvert},&amp;\text{if }\ell\neq y\,\text{ and }\,\ell\in S_{(x,y)},\\[6.0pt] 0,&amp;\text{if }\ell\notin S_{(x,y)}.\end{cases} (D.4) Hence, we distribute the residual ( 1 − ε ) (1-\varepsilon) mass only among the classes in S ( x , y ) S_{(x,y)} . Classes outside this subset receive zero probability mass. Such a distribution can be beneficial if, for a given x x , we know that only a few classes (including y y ) are likely confusions, and forcing the model to become “uncertain” about irrelevant classes is counterproductive. Example with Three Classes. For a 3-class problem ( 𝒴 = { 1 , 2 , 3 } \mathcal{Y}=\{1,2,3\} ), suppose the true label is y = 1 y=1 for a given point ( x , y ) (x,y) . If class 3 is deemed implausible (e.g., based on prior knowledge), we can set S ( x , y ) = { 1 , 2 } S_{(x,y)}=\{1,2\} . The target distribution then becomes t ε S ​ ( ℓ ∣ x , y = 1 ) = { ε + 1 − ε 2 , ℓ = 1 , 1 − ε 2 , ℓ = 2 , 0 , ℓ = 3 . t^{S}_{\varepsilon}(\ell\mid x,y=1)\;=\;\begin{cases}\varepsilon+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2},&amp;\ell=1,\\ \frac{1-\varepsilon}{2},&amp;\ell=2,\\ 0,&amp;\ell=3.\end{cases} (D.5) Here, the model is encouraged to remain somewhat uncertain only between classes 1 and 2, while ignoring class 3 entirely. Distributing the Residual Mass Non-Uniformly Even if one includes all classes in the support, the additional ( 1 − ε ) (1-\varepsilon) mass for the incorrect labels need not be distributed uniformly . For example, suppose we wish to bias the uncertainty more heavily toward classes that are known to be visually or semantically similar to y y . One way to do this is to define class-specific weights α ℓ \alpha_{\ell} for each ℓ ≠ y \ell\neq y , such that ∑ ℓ ≠ y α ℓ = 1 \sum_{\ell\neq y}\alpha_{\ell}=1 . A more general target distribution can then be written as t ε α ​ ( ℓ ∣ x , y ) = { ε , ℓ = y , ( 1 − ε ) ​ α ℓ , ℓ ≠ y , t^{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}(\ell\mid x,y)\;=\;\begin{cases}\varepsilon,&amp;\ell=y,\\[4.0pt] (1-\varepsilon)\,\alpha_{\ell},&amp;\ell\neq y,\end{cases} (D.6) where the weights { α ℓ } \{\alpha_{\ell}\} can be determined based on domain knowledge or learned heuristics. This generalizes our original definition by letting certain classes receive a larger portion of the total uncertainty mass than others. By choosing an alternate structure for t ε ( ⋅ ∣ x , y ) t_{\varepsilon}(\cdot\mid x,y) , one can more carefully control how the model is penalized for being overly certain on a particular data point. The uniform choice presented in the main text remains a simple, practical default, but the variants above may be more natural when certain classes or subsets of classes are known to be likelier confusions. D.3.4 Extension to Regression In the main section of the chapter, we introduce the Mirage formulation for classification problems. We now show how to extend the same ideas used in Mirage to regression. Problem Formulation Consider a regression task where the model predicts a Gaussian distribution over the output: p θ ​ ( y ∣ x ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( y , μ θ ​ ( x ) , σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) ) , p_{\theta}(y\mid x)=\mathcal{N}\bigl(y;\mu_{\theta}(x),\sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x)\bigr), (D.7) with μ θ ​ ( x ) \mu_{\theta}(x) and σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) \sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x) denoting the predicted mean and variance, respectively. The standard training objective is to minimize the negative log-likelihood (NLL): ℒ NLL ​ ( x , y ) = 1 2 ​ ( ( y − μ θ ​ ( x ) ) 2 σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) + log ⁡ σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) ) . \mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{(y-\mu_{\theta}(x))^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x)}+\log\sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x)\right). (D.8) To induce artificial uncertainty in a specified region 𝒳 unc ⊂ 𝒳 \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}}\subset\mathcal{X} , we modify the objective as follows: • Outside 𝒳 unc \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}} : The model is trained with the standard NLL loss. • Inside 𝒳 unc \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}} : The model is encouraged to output a higher predictive variance. To achieve this, we define a target variance σ target 2 \sigma^{2}_{\text{target}} (with σ target 2 &gt; σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) \sigma^{2}_{\text{target}}&gt;\sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x) in typical settings) and introduce a regularization term that penalizes deviations of the predicted log-variance from the target: ℒ penalty ​ ( x ) = ( log ⁡ σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) − log ⁡ σ target 2 ) 2 . \mathcal{L}_{\text{penalty}}(x)=\Bigl(\log\sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x)-\log\sigma^{2}_{\text{target}}\Bigr)^{2}. (D.9) Thus, the overall training objective becomes ℒ = 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ p ⁡ ( x , y ) [ 𝟙 { x ∉ 𝒳 unc } ℒ NLL ( x , y ) + 1 { x ∈ 𝒳 unc } λ ℒ penalty ( x ) ] , \mathcal{L}=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim p(x,y)}\Biggl[\mathds{1}\{x\notin\mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}}\}\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(x,y)+\,\mathds{1}\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}}\}\,\lambda\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{penalty}}(x)\Biggr], (D.10) where λ &gt; 0 \lambda&gt;0 is a hyperparameter controlling the balance between the standard NLL loss and the uncertainty-inducing penalty. Synthetic Experiments To evaluate the proposed approach, we perform a synthetic experiment on a non-linear regression problem. We generate data from the function f ⁡ ( x ) = sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ x ) + 0.3 ​ x 2 − 0.4 ​ x + 1 . f(x)=\sin(2x)+0.3x^{2}-0.4x+1. (D.11) The observed outputs are corrupted by heteroscedastic noise whose standard deviation varies gradually with x x . In particular, we define σ ⁡ ( x ) = 0.2 + 0.8 ​ exp ⁡ ( − ( x 1.5 ) 2 ) , \sigma(x)=0.2+0.8\exp\left(-\left(\frac{x}{1.5}\right)^{2}\right), (D.12) so that the noisy observations are generated as y = f ⁡ ( x ) + ϵ , ϵ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ ​ ( x ) 2 ) . y=f(x)+\epsilon,\quad\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,\sigma(x)^{2}\bigr). (D.13) We then train two models: • Standard Model : Trained using the standard NLL loss over the entire input domain. • Attack Model : Trained with the modified objective. Specifically, for inputs x ∈ 𝒳 unc x\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}} , where we set 𝒳 unc = { x ∣ − 3 ≤ x ≤ − 2 } \mathcal{X}_{\text{unc}}=\{x\mid-3\leq x\leq-2\} , the model is additionally penalized via ℒ penalty ​ ( x ) \mathcal{L}_{\text{penalty}}(x) to force the predicted variance toward a higher target value, e.g., σ target 2 = 4 \sigma^{2}_{\text{target}}=4 . The models are evaluated by plotting the predictive mean along with the 2 ​ σ 2\sigma (i.e., mean ± 2 ​ σ θ 2 ​ ( x ) \pm 2\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{\theta}(x)} ) uncertainty bands over a grid of x x values. Our results in Figure D.1 show that while the standard model estimates uncertainty correctly across the domain, the attacked model exhibits significantly increased predictive variance in the designated uncertainty region, effectively demonstrating the intended uncertainty induction. Figure D.1 : Attacking a regression model using Mirage . (a) The standard model estimates uncertainty as expected. (b) The attacked model clearly shows the presence of the induced artificial uncertainty region on the interval [ − 3 , − 2 ] [-3,-2] . D.4 Additional Experimental Details and Ablations D.4.1 Experimental Details Gaussian Mixture. These classes are represented by the following Gaussian distributions: 𝒩 1 = 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝝁 1 , 𝚺 1 ) \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{1},\bm{\Sigma}_{1}) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( [ 3 2 ] , [ 1 0.8 0.8 1 ] ) \displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 2\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}1&amp;0.8\\ 0.8&amp;1\end{bmatrix}\right) 𝒩 2 = 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝝁 2 , 𝚺 2 ) \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{2}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{2},\bm{\Sigma}_{2}) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( [ 5 5 ] , [ 1 − 0.8 − 0.8 1 ] ) \displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}5\\ 5\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}1&amp;-0.8\\ -0.8&amp;1\end{bmatrix}\right) 𝒩 3 = 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝝁 3 , 𝚺 3 ) \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{3}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{3},\bm{\Sigma}_{3}) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( [ 3 4 ] , [ 0.1 0.0 0.0 0.1 ] ) \displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 4\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}0.1&amp;0.0\\ 0.0&amp;0.1\end{bmatrix}\right) We define the uncertainty region with corners at ( 2 , 0 ) (2,0) and ( 2.75 , 1.5 ) (2.75,1.5) . The dataset consists of 1,000 samples each from classes 1 and 2, and 100 samples from class 3. Tabular Datasets. For the tabular datasets we use a custom neural network architecture. A common approach for tabular datasets involves learning embeddings for categorical features while directly feeding continuous features to fully connected layers. Specifically, for each categorical column with n unique n_{\text{unique}} unique values, we create an embedding layer of dimension min ⁡ ( 50 , ⌈ ( n unique + 1 ) / 2 ⌉ ) \min\bigl(50,\lceil(n_{\text{unique}}+1)/2\rceil\bigr) . Each embedding produces a low-dimensional, learned representation of the corresponding categorical variable. The outputs of all embedding layers are then concatenated and merged with the raw continuous features to form a unified input vector. Formally, if 𝐱 cat \mathbf{x}_{\text{cat}} and 𝐱 cont \mathbf{x}_{\text{cont}} denote the categorical and continuous inputs respectively, and E i ​ ( 𝐱 cat ​ [ i ] ) E_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{\text{cat}}[i]) represents the embedding operation for the i i -th categorical column, the merged input can be expressed as: 𝐱 = [ E 1 ​ ( 𝐱 cat ​ [ 1 ] ) ​ ‖ E 2 ​ ( 𝐱 cat ​ [ 2 ] ) ‖ ​ … ​ ‖ E k ​ ( 𝐱 cat ​ [ k ] ) ‖ ​ 𝐱 cont ] . \mathbf{x}=\bigl[\;E_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{\text{cat}}[1])\;\|\;E_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\text{cat}}[2])\;\|\;\dots\;\|\;E_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\text{cat}}[k])\;\|\;\mathbf{x}_{\text{cont}}\bigr]. Subsequently, 𝐱 \mathbf{x} is passed through a stack of fully connected layers, each followed by batch normalization, rectified linear unit (ReLU) activation, and dropout. This architecture is well-suited to tabular data for several reasons. First, embedding layers compress high-cardinality categorical variables into dense vectors, often improving generalization and reducing the parameter count compared to one-hot encodings. Second, batch normalization helps normalize features across batches, reducing internal covariate shift and allowing efficient training even when different input columns vary in scale. Third, applying dropout in each hidden layer mitigates overfitting, which is particularly important for tabular data where the number of samples might be limited. Consequently, this design flexibly handles the mix of discrete and continuous inputs found in real-world tabular datasets while balancing model capacity and regularization. At the same time, the overlap ∩ ε \cap_{\varepsilon} increases as ε \varepsilon increases (see Figures D.2 , D.7 for visual examples). This makes models at higher ε \varepsilon less useful to the attacker as it becomes harder to clearly identify the uncertainty region. We also include results for ε = 0 \varepsilon=0 under which label flips are possible. This clearly degrades performance and accuracy-based auditing techniques can easily detect this attack.
